ACKNOWLEDGEMENTS



Mathematics Curriculum 1.1
ᐃᑲᔪᖅᓯᒪᔪᑦ
IKAJUQSIMAJUT
ACKNOWLEDGEMENTS 
The Nunavut Department of Education would like to extend its sincere 
gratitude to the large number of people who have worked hard to develop 
this made-in-Nunavut K-6 Curriculum. The process occurred over many 
years and included Elders, educators, department staff, community 
members, and subject matter specialists, as well as those involved in editing 
and design. 
The Inuit Qaujimajatuqangit Principles are not only embedded in the 
curriculum but also truly facilitated its development. Nunavut Elders 
contributed greatly to the Inuit Qaujimajatuqangit Education Framework 
for Nunavut Curriculum (2007), which formed the foundation for this 
curriculum. Extensive consultations and meetings with educators, Elders, 
and other stakeholders, prior to the writing of actual learning outcomes, 
helped create the framework and the content for the curriculum. In the 
holistic tradition of Inuit culture, there was close collaboration among 
the writers of the different subject areas to ensure that the educational 
experience of Nunavut children would be fully integrated. 
The valuable efforts, knowledge, skills, and experience of all contributors 
have helped to fulfill one of Nunavut’s fundamental goals as a territory: to 
provide our students with a unique Nunavut-made curriculum – one in 
which our learners can see themselves and their communities and which 
will influence them long into the future. 



ᓇᓗᓇᐃᖅᓯᔾᔪᑦ 
NALUNAIQSIJJUT
PREFACE

ᓇᓗᓇᐃᖅᓯᔾᔪᑦ
NALUNAIQSIJJUT
PREFACE
The Nunavut Kindergarten–Grade 6 Mathematics Curriculum nurtures 
students’ holistic development. It aims to foster intellectual growth through 
a progression of Learning Outcomes that develop students’ number and 
spatial sense, problem-solving skills, and critical thinking. Through handson learning and real-world applications, it highlights the importance of 
using mathematical concepts, builds emotional resilience, and strengthens 
cultural identity while laying a foundation for lifelong learning.
Children naturally love math! When schools begin in the fall, and Nunavut 
newspaper reporters ask elementary students what their favourite subject 
is, a common response is always math. Mathematics provides an intuitive 
and logical path of discovery for children as they make sense of their 
surroundings through numbers, shapes, and spaces. 
In Nunavut, mathematics education integrates Inuit Qaujimajatuqangit 
(IQ) Principles to ensure traditional knowledge and cultural contexts are 
embedded into the curriculum and learning is meaningful and relevant. 
In particular, three IQ Principles naturally reside in the mathematics 
classroom: 
• Pilimmaksarniq: The concept of skills and knowledge acquisition 
(hands-on learning).
• Qanuqtuurunnarniq: The concept of being resourceful to solve problems 
(problem-solving).
• Piliriqatigiingniq: The concept of developing a collaborative relationship 
or working together for a common purpose (group work).
Each of these principles informs the pedagogy of instruction and daily 
routines to link mathematical skills to past and present cultural practices. 
Measurement, patterns, and construction, as well as data analysis and the 
use of variables, help students to experience and understand mathematics 
rather than merely memorize it.

Mathematics Curriculum 2.1
Teaching and learning math require an open mindset. Learning involves 
making mistakes, trying again, and not giving up. Perseverance and 
practice lead to mastery. These Inuit values are the basis of problemsolving, strong personal character, and capable human beings. The 
Nunavut Kindergarten–Grade 6 Mathematics Curriculum nurtures these 
values, enabling students to confidently meet challenges and opportunities 
inside and outside of the classroom.
Jim Kreuger
Educator/Program Coordinator 
Formerly of Baker Lake, Nunavut

TAKUQQUJJIJJUT

ILINNIALISAAQTUNIT QUTTINGNILINGNUT 6
INTRODUCTION TO THE NUNAVUT CURRICULUM,
KINDERGARTEN TO GRADE 6
As stated in the Inuit Qaujimajatuqangit Education Framework for Nunavut 
Curriculum, “schooling in Nunavut should provide support to students in all 
areas of their development so that they can achieve personal goals, become 
well-equipped to contribute and serve their families and communities, 
demonstrate leadership and healthy attitudes, and be able to actively 
participate and contribute as Nunavut takes on new roles in the global 
community.”¹ To achieve this vision for education in Nunavut, it is imperative 
that the curriculum in all subject areas promotes holistic critical thinking 
and encourages continuous learning that can be transferred across contexts. 
It must also promote inclusion and provide opportunities to differentiate 
learning experiences to meet the needs of all students. The updated Nunavut 
Curriculum Framework will provide a common structure for the curriculum 
in each subject area and support this holistic, cross-curricular approach to 
teaching and learning.
—

for Nunavut Curriculum (2007), 19

The Curriculum Foundations —Nunavusiutit, Iqqaqqaukkaringniq, 
Uqausiliriniq, and Aulajaaqtut.—are at the centre of the curriculum in each 
subject area. According to the Inuit Qaujimajatuqangit Education Framework 
for Nunavut Curriculum, the foundations “are generic in nature, transcend 
subject-specific content areas, and result in the application and transfer of 
specific learning to new contexts and situations. This approach to learning is 
designed to focus on the development of complex intellectual (metacognitive) 
skills and lead students to transformational ways of thinking and processing. 
This type of approach helps students understand the connections between 
various learnings and the strategies that lead to successful application of 
learning in new contexts.”² Within the Curriculum Foundations are the 
subject areas. These are areas of learning organized as discrete learning 
disciplines and taught in systemic ways over time.

Mathematics Curriculum 3.3
—
Notes
3. G. Wiggins and J. McTighe, Understanding by Design, expanded 2nd ed. 
(Alexandria, VA: ASCD, 2005).
The aims and principles of the Curriculum Foundations inform the Major 
Understandings in each subject area. Major Understandings are broad statements 
that reflect the essential principles and core ideas within a subject area that can 
be transferred across different areas of learning.³ These Major Understandings 
anchor the curriculum in the purpose for learning in each subject area and 
the values of Inuit Qaujimajatuqangit (IQ). They act as a lens through which 
teachers can make decisions about planning, instruction, and assessment. 
The Major Understandings are explored through the Learning Strands, which 
represent the key concepts and processes within each subject area and the 
relationships among them. The Learning Strands remain consistent across grades 
and will encourage a richer exploration of how these concepts and processes 
work together over time. Within each Learning Strand, there are Learning 
Concepts and grade-specific Learning Outcomes. The Learning Concepts outline 
the constructs, processes, and patterns within each Learning Strand. These 
concepts provide a structure through which students can organize and connect 
the Learning Outcomes. Learning Outcomes define the key knowledge, skills, 
and understandings students will develop at each grade level. These become 
the building blocks that support deeper conceptual understanding.
In addition, each grade level has Guiding Questions. The Guiding Questions 
will help link the Learning Concepts and the Learning Outcomes back to 
the Curriculum Foundations. By building connections among the knowledge, 
skills, and understandings they have acquired, students will be able to explore 
the principles of the Curriculum Foundations in age-appropriate ways.
Teachers can use the Guiding Questions in the following ways:
• In daily classroom activities and discussions
• As part of assessment tasks
• To plan units or themes
• To inspire inquiry-based projects

3.6 Introduction
ᑕᑯᖅᑯᔾᔨᔾᔪᑦ ᓈᓴᐅᓯᕆᓂᕐᒥᒃ

TAKUQQUJJIJJUTIT NAASAUSIRINIRMIK 
ILINIARUTINIK
INTRODUCTION TO THE MATHEMATICS CURRICULUM
What is the Mathematics Curriculum?
Mathematics is the study of numbers, shapes, and space. Mathematics 
is everywhere, and everything can be evaluated and described using 
mathematics. It allows us to quantify elements of the world, make reasonable 
predictions, explain our surroundings, build, and create.
The Nunavut Mathematics Curriculum aims to help students become 
increasingly experienced in Mathematics as they go from Kindergarten 
to Grade 6. They will develop a body of knowledge, procedures, and skills 
that they can use to think and communicate in a variety of ways, to solve 
problems, and to recognize situations in which Mathematics can be applied. 
A strong understanding of Mathematics allows for students to interact with 
the world in a way they can quantify. 
Rationale
Mathematics is essential for making sense of the world around us. When we 
observe and engage with Mathematics early in life, we begin to see connections to 
our daily routines and understand the role math plays in how we solve simple 
and complex problems. Through the Nunavut Kindergarten–Grade 6 Mathematics 
Curriculum, students will learn how to see mathematical concepts represented 
in their environment. They will begin to view problem-solving as a journey 
that requires multiple steps, beginning with choosing and applying appropriate 
tools and strategies, and then persevering toward solutions. Students will 
learn to problem-solve both individually and collaboratively, and they will 
explore the benefits of analyzing, visualizing, and exploring problems from 
multiple angles. Through this curriculum, students will also develop an 
understanding of estimation and recognize how reasoning can be applied to 
daily life. This exploration of Mathematics will enable students to view math as 
a representation of the qualities and quantities within their environment.

Mathematics Curriculum 3.7
Goals
Through the Mathematics Curriculum, students from Kindergarten to 
Grade 6 will:
• Develop mathematical skills and processes and apply them together to 
solve problems
• Learn to view their environment through a mathematical lens and 
connect mathematical concepts to their lives and the world around them
• Develop number fluency and a strong foundation of the composition 
and decomposition of numbers in order to apply these to broader 
problem-solving contexts
• Develop strategies and tools to navigate and accurately solve 
mathematical problems related to patterns, numbers, measurement, 
probability, statistics, shape, and space
• Persevere, practise, and apply heavy thinking to build, solve, and create 
in mathematical ways
• Understand Mathematics as a way of seeing and doing rather than 
hearing and memorizing
Skills for Mathematics
Mathematical skills are important to help students learn and apply 
Mathematics. This curriculum builds foundational skills that are essential for 
math proficiency. Students must be given the opportunity to regularly use and 
practise these skills. These skills are:
Estimation
Estimation means to find an approximate answer, or to find a value 
close to the right answer. Through the grades, this curriculum builds 
estimation skills, with outcomes dedicated to students applying strategies 
of estimation. Beginning in the early grades, students will learn to use 
benchmark numbers (5, 10, etc.), personal referents, and find patterns. As 
they move into later grades, students begin to use learned number patterns, 
such as those in multiplication and division, and strategies, such as using 
compatible numbers, doubling, and rounding to estimate efficiently. 

3.8 Introduction
Critical thinking
Critical thinking means to use high-level reasoning skills to make 
assessments and form conclusions. This may include asking questions, 
reviewing facts or evidence, analyzing and interpreting information, and 
forming supported judgments. 
Numerical calculation
Numerical calculation means taking a larger problem and breaking it down 
into parts to solve it using algorithms and operations. Students begin with 
the basics of operations, moving into an understanding of place value and 
how they can use it to perform calculations easier. Students build on their 
mental math strategies through the grades, enabling them to break larger 
problems into smaller parts that they can solve.
Analyzing data
Analyzing data refers to logically collecting, sorting, organizing, reviewing, 
and interpreting data in order to make informed conclusions or decisions. 
Through the Statistics and Probability strand in particular, students 
slowly build understanding of data through simple collection methods, 
such as tally charts, and learn to display and read data in simple graphs. 
In later grades, students begin to chart and read data in more complex 
representations, such as line graphs, double-bar graphs, and coordinate planes.
Measurement
To measure is to quantify an object. This may include its size, mass, 
volume, perimeter, area, length (height and width), or amount. Students 
begin by understanding sizes, shapes, objects, and non-standard units of 
measurement. They then build on this knowledge to understand and apply 
standard units.

Mathematics Curriculum 3.9
Mathematical processes
Successful Mathematics learning requires students to have confidence in 
their ability to do and make sense of Mathematics. Students develop a 
positive attitude toward Mathematics by persevering through challenging 
tasks, making connections, finding meaning, and applying their knowledge. 
Mathematical processes refer to the thinking tools that students develop 
over time to see Mathematics as useful, applicable, and manageable. Students 
must encounter these processes on a regular basis during instruction in 
order to fully realize their potential. Throughout the Nunavut Mathematics 
Curriculum, these processes are consistently incorporated and built upon so 
that students develop them over the years. The outcomes encourage students 
to make connections to personal examples and daily life, allowing them 
to see Mathematics and its uses. Students are also encouraged to explore 
when learning Mathematics, finding various entry points, using multiple 
strategies, and trying different solutions. The processes of Mathematics are at 
the heart of being a mathematician, and through this curriculum, students 
will see how finding solutions requires heavy thinking, experimentation, and 
perseverance. 
Students will develop the following mathematical processes through this 
curriculum: 
Problem-solving 
This refers to the ability to analyze a problem and then create and 
implement potential solutions. Problem-solving is central to learning 
Mathematics. When problem-solving is required, the answer or solution 
is not immediately apparent. Students must see the problem, consider 
potential solutions, implement these ideas, assess if they have led to the 
solution, and reassess and retry when they do not. They must practise 
learning from mistakes. It is through problem-solving that students can 
build on previous experiences and incorporate new learning. Students must 
organize their knowledge into a coherent whole by connecting new ideas to 
what they have previously learned. 

3.10 Introduction
Mathematical reasoning 
This refers to the ability to analyze a mathematical problem, think 
critically, and develop logical arguments. It is the act of heavy thinking. It 
involves students developing confidence in their ability to make and test 
conjectures, form generalizations, and justify conclusions based on what is 
already known or assumed to be true. It is through reasoning that students 
make sense of the Mathematics they engage with and transfer their 
knowledge to new situations. 
Representing
This refers to the ability to demonstrate mathematical ideas and 
relationships and model a variety of situations in multiple ways. Through 
learning various forms of representation (such as pictures, objects, and 
symbols), students are better able to understand mathematical concepts 
and relationships, communicate their thinking and understanding, make 
connections among related concepts, and use Mathematics to model realworld situations.
Communicating mathematical ideas to others 
This refers to the ability to use mathematical language to demonstrate 
mathematical ideas clearly and concisely. Communication is important 
for helping students clarify, reinforce, and modify their ideas and beliefs 
about Mathematics. Learning to communicate effectively in Mathematics 
promotes interaction and exploration in the classroom as students learn 
to make connections, use mathematical terminology, and create links 
between their own language and ideas. It is important to consider that 
students may communicate in their preferred language, and all languages 
are valued means of communicating mathematical thinking.

Mathematics Curriculum 3.11
Connecting mathematical ideas 
This refers to the ability to see and make crucial links among various 
mathematical ideas, between ideas in Mathematics and other subjects 
(such as Arts, Science, and Social Studies), and between the math they are 
learning in school and their lives outside school. This helps students make 
sense of what they are learning and begin to view the subject as useful, 
relevant, and integrated.

3.12 Introduction
ᐋᖅᑭᒃᓱᖅᓯᒪᓂᖏᑦ ᓈᓴᐅᓯᕆᓂᕐᒥᒃ

AAQQIKSUQSIMANINGIT NAASAUSIRINIRMIK 
ILINNIARUTIT
ORGANIZATION OF THE MATHEMATICS 
CURRICULUM

Mathematics Curriculum 3.13
Curriculum Foundation
Iqqaqqaukkaringniq
Major Understandings
1. Numbers help us describe, order, measure, quantify, and estimate so we 
can make sense of our world and solve everyday problems.
2. Recognizing and creating patterns allows us to make predictions, describe 
and justify relationships, and make connections in our daily lives.
3. An understanding of shape and space allows us to describe and 
represent objects and navigate our environment.
4. Collecting, representing, and interpreting information help us answer 
important questions and make informed decisions that benefit our 
families and communities.
Learning Strands
Number | Patterns and Relations | Shape and Space | Statistics and Probability
Learning Outcomes
Learning Concepts
Number: Number sense; Operations
Patterns and Relations: Patterns; Expressions and equations
Shape and Space: Measurement; 2D shapes and 3D objects; Transformations
Statistics and Probability: Data analysis; Chance and uncertainty

3.14 Introduction
Curriculum Foundation
The Mathematics Curriculum is part of the Iqqaqqaukkaringniq Curriculum 
Foundation. The principles of the Iqqaqqaukkaringniq Curriculum focus on 
heavy thinking, curiosity, experimentation, leadership, and learning from 
mistakes.
Major Understandings
The Mathematics Curriculum informs the Major Understandings within 
the curriculum that continue from Kindergarten to Grade 6. These Major 
Understandings reflect the essential learning within the subject area and can 
act as a lens through which teachers can make decisions about planning, 
instruction, and assessment. The Major Understandings for Mathematics are:
• Numbers help us describe, order, measure, quantify, and estimate so 
we can make sense of our world and solve everyday problems.
• Recognizing and creating patterns allows us to make predictions, 
describe and justify relationships, and make connections in our daily 
lives.
• An understanding of shape and space allows us to describe and 
represent objects and navigate our environment.
• Collecting, representing, and interpreting information helps us answer 
important questions and make informed decisions that benefit our 
families and communities.

Mathematics Curriculum 3.15
Learning Strands and Learning Concepts
The Mathematics Curriculum has been organized by grade, and then further 
broken down into four Learning Strands: Number, Patterns and Relations, 
Shape and Space, and Statistics and Probability. The Learning Outcomes 
within each Learning Strand have been further organized into Learning 
Concepts. The Learning Outcomes within each Learning Strand have been 
organized according to their Learning Concept so that there is a clear 
progression of student learning from Kindergarten to Grade 6.
When working toward proficiency in Mathematics, students need to develop a 
deep understanding of numbers: what they are, how they can be represented, 
and how they relate to each other. For this reason, the Number strand forms 
the foundation of this curriculum, and the corresponding Learning Strands 
are grounded in an understanding that students are developing a sense of 
numbers and operations. Numbers are used to describe the attributes studied 
in the Shape and Space strand, to represent the principles and connections in 
the Patterns and Relations strand, and to explore and make sense of data in 
the Statistics and Probability strand. Teachers are encouraged to provide time, 
resources, and attention to a deep development of working with numbers.
Number
The Number strand is the foundation for understanding and working 
with numbers. The focus of this strand is on number sense, which is 
the development of an intuition about numbers and their relationships. 
Through this strand, students will develop and apply strategies to count 
and represent numbers, and they will explore the limitless ways in which 
numbers can be composed, decomposed, and utilized. They will also learn 
how to apply a range of computational strategies, explore and visualize 
numbers in a variety of contexts, and make connections between operations. 
Developing a strong sense of numbers will allow students to move beyond 
standard algorithms and relate concepts of numbers to real-life experiences.
• Number sense
Number sense refers to an understanding of numbers. This includes 
identifying and representing numbers, counting, composing and 
decomposing numbers, and understanding how numbers can be 
manipulated.

3.16 Introduction
• Operations
The four operations in Mathematics are addition, subtraction, 
multiplication, and division. These are used to solve mathematical 
questions and problems.
Patterns and Relations
Developing an understanding of patterns is at the heart of learning 
Mathematics. Through this strand, students will identify, create, and 
describe a variety of patterns, and produce generalizations about 
relationships within and among patterns. Students will learn that patterns 
and numerical relationships can be represented in a variety of ways, and 
they will begin to represent mathematical ideas through simple numerical 
expressions and equations.
• Patterns
A pattern is something that repeats and follows a rule. This Learning 
Concept works on identifying, replicating, and creating patterns. 
• Expressions and equations
In this Learning Concept, students use their understanding of patterns 
to begin to solve for unknown variables. They use pattern knowledge 
to make predictions and determinations about missing values and 
variables.
Shape and Space
In the Shape and Space strand, students will learn to interpret and reflect 
on their physical environment. They will develop spatial awareness and 
reasoning skills that will enable them to visualize and navigate the 
world around them. Students will explore and solve problems involving 
2D figures and 3D objects; use appropriate standard and non-standard 
units to measure; and make predictions and interpretations about their 
environment.

Mathematics Curriculum 3.17
• Measurement
Students learn to use non-standard and standard units of measurement 
to describe the size, length, area, perimeter, mass, and capacity of objects.
• 2D shapes and 3D objects
Students learn to identify, describe, compare, and create 2D shapes 
and 3D objects. Through an understanding of the properties of shapes 
and objects, students can apply this knowledge in conjunction with 
measurement, transformations, and STEAM (Science, Technology, 
Engineering, Arts, and Mathematics) learning.
• Transformations
Students learn to understand symmetry and to describe the movement of 
shapes and objects. 
Statistics and Probability
In this strand, students will learn how to collect, organize, and interpret 
data. They will learn how to look for patterns in data and how to use 
this information to make informed predictions and decisions. Students 
will develop an understanding of how uncertainty is inherent to making 
predictions and recognize how the quality of data directly affects the 
quality of the interpretations. Students will assess the probability of events 
and learn how managing and determining probability empower us to work 
together to solve existing or potential problems.
• Data analysis
Students will learn how to collect, organize, represent, evaluate, and 
interpret data in order to make predictions and decisions. 
• Chance and uncertainty
Students will learn how to determine the probability of events by 
looking at the theoretical and experimental probability. They will 
represent probability in different ways, including through words, 
fractions, decimals, and percentages.

Additional notes
The “Additional notes” section provides other information about the Learning 
Outcomes, including definitions of any potentially unfamiliar vocabulary.

more information.
• “Begins to”
This is added to some of the Learning Outcomes and their descriptors to 
signify that students should be attempting to demonstrate the observable 
behaviours and sharing their knowledge connected to this Learning 
Outcome throughout the year, but that continued support and modelling 
are required. 
• “With support”
This has been added to some of the Learning Outcome descriptors to 
inform educators that students will still require support to demonstrate 
their knowledge or skills. Support may include visuals, prompts, reminders, 
and non-verbal gestures.

3.20 Introduction
ᐃᓄᐃᑦ ᖃᐅᔨᒪᔭᑐᖃᖏᑦ ᓈᓴᐅᓯᕆᓂᕐᒥ
INUIT QAUJIMAJATUQANGIT NAASAUSIRINIRMI
INUIT QAUJIMAJATUQANGIT IN MATHEMATICS
The learning of Mathematics often involves making mistakes, trying again, 
applying procedures in different ways, and building skills over time to become 
proficient. In this way, Mathematics also helps develop Inuit values that 
lead to strength of character, such as perseverance, practise, and mastery. 
All the IQ Principles are incorporated in the Nunavut Mathematics 
Curriculum; however, three principles in particular may be highlighted:
• Pilimmaksarniq 
The concept of skills and knowledge acquisition
• Qanuqtuurunnarniq 
The concept of being resourceful to solve problems
• Piliriqatigiingniq 
The concept of developing a collaborative relationship or working 
together for a common purpose
As their Mathematics skills and processes build through the grades, 
students will gain knowledge of Mathematics, steadily building their skills 
and understanding. Students are also encouraged to access prior knowledge 
to help them when learning new concepts. They must also learn how 
to choose and apply their knowledge in order to solve problems. When 
students see the value of Mathematics in their everyday life, they can 
also apply it to common goals and see how Mathematics can be a tool in 
collaborative discussion, reasoning, and planning. 

Mathematics Curriculum 3.21
ᓄᓇᕗᒻᒥ ᓈᓴᐅᓯᕆᓂᕐᒥᒃ 
ᐃᓕᓐᓂᐊᒐᒃᓴᓕᐅᕐᓗᓂ 
ᐃᖅᑲᐅᒪᔭᐅᔭᕆᐊᓖᑦ
NUNAVUMMI NAASAUSIRINIRMIK 
ILINNIAGAKSALIURLUNI IQQAUMAJAUJARIALIIT
NUNAVUT MATHEMATICS CURRICULUM
CONSIDERATIONS
Hands-on Mathematics
Learning Mathematics concepts and skills using manipulatives and 
other physical tools gives students the concrete learning experiences they 
need to make sense of Mathematics ideas through exploration and selfdiscovery. Students must be given opportunities to play an active role in 
the Mathematics classroom. Whether through activities such as counting 
and estimating with bottle caps, rocks, and beans, or using manipulatives 
such as base-10 blocks, Cuisenaire rods, or geoboards, hands-on learning 
can help students more readily understand mathematical concepts and 
boost confidence in their abilities as problem-solvers. A hands-on approach 
to learning Mathematics has been proven to increase students’ academic 
achievement and understanding, as the manipulation of objects helps 
make abstract knowledge more concrete and clear, giving visual and tactile 
illustrations of concepts. Through hands-on approaches, students engage in 
real-life illustrations, represent problem situations in a variety of ways, and 
recognize when some ways of representing a problem may be more helpful 
than others. This method is student-centred as it allows the learner to see, 
touch, and manipulate objects while learning, making Mathematics a subject 
of seeing and doing rather than hearing or memorizing. Teachers can refer to 
the Mathematics Educator Handbook for more information.

3.22 Introduction
Making Mathematics Meaningful
In order for Mathematics to feel relevant and applicable to students, it is 
important for students to see how Mathematics applies to the world around 
them. Effective questioning and rich problem-solving that relate to real-life 
scenarios help students understand the application of Mathematics and gives 
them the confidence to transfer skills and knowledge from the concrete to 
the abstract. Students should understand that they are building a toolbox to 
solve daily problems.
Students’ community and ways of knowing play an integral role in learning, 
and in the meaning they attach to that learning. Traditional teaching, often 
based on observing and listening to stories, is a very rich and vital part of 
Inuit ways of knowing. Children need to “relate their learning to their family 
and surroundings and to be given opportunities to try different things.”4
Connecting the Mathematics that students are learning to Inuit ways of 
knowing serves to enhance the teaching and learning of the subject as 
students begin to use mathematical knowledge, skills, and attitudes to make 
sense of the world around them. Engaging in problem-solving experiences 
that are connected to the land, stories, local practices, and perspectives 
relevant to the local community helps connect education to students’ lived 
experiences. 
—
Notes
4. Northwest Territories Department of Education, Culture and Employment, 
Inuuqatigiit: The Curriculum from the Inuit Perspective (1996), 10

Mathematics Curriculum 3.23
ᐃᓕᓐᓂᐊᖅᑎᑦᑎᓂᖅ ᐃᓕᓐᓂᐊᕈᑎᓕᒫᑦ 
ᐊᑐᕐᓗᒋᑦ
ILINNIAQTITTINIQ ILINNIARUTILIMAAT ATURLUGIT
TEACHING FROM A CROSS-CURRICULAR 
PRESPECTIVE
A cross-curricular approach to curriculum planning helps make learning more 
meaningful for students. It promotes a holistic approach that provides students 
with multiple ways to reinforce and demonstrate their knowledge and skills in 
various contexts. The Nunavut Kindergarten–Grade 6 Mathematics Curriculum
allows students to acquire new knowledge by building on previously learned 
concepts and connecting their learning to other subject areas. There are strong 
connections that can be made to the Science and Arts curricula through the 
use of STEAM principles. STEAM stands for Science, Technology, Engineering, 
Arts, and Mathematics. These subjects work together, often overlapping in 
many areas of the world. STEAM learning shows how real-life problem-solving 
and application often involve combining skills, concepts, and ideas from a 
variety of disciplines. When students learn in an interdisciplinary manner, 
with emphasis on exploring concepts together instead of independently, they 
can build their critical thinking and inquiry skills. Teachers are encouraged 
to look for opportunities to connect Mathematics to other subjects, engaging 
in meaningful tasks that allow students to access a range of knowledge and 
showing them how Mathematics aligns and applies to daily problem-solving. 
For more information on cross-curricular teaching and STEAM, refer to the 
Mathematics Educator Handbook.

3.24 Introduction
ᖃᕆᓴᐅᔭᓕᕆᓗᓂ ᐃᓕᓐᓂᐊᕈᑎᑦ
QARISAUJALIRILUNI ILINNIARUTIT
USE OF INFORMATION AND COMMUNICATION 
TECHNOLOGY
Incorporating technology into the Mathematics Curriculum can create a rich 
learning environment, thereby allowing students to see and interact with 
mathematical concepts and processes in a variety of formats. Technology can 
contribute to collaborative learning experiences that support problem-solving 
and flexible thinking. However, technology should be used alongside, and not 
instead of, instruction, hands-on manipulatives, discussions, and activities. 
Calculators, for example, can be used to check estimates or perform long and 
complex calculations so that students can focus on problem structure in the 
development of their problem-solving abilities. There are a variety of apps and 
software that offer productive ways for students to engage with visuals and 
digital manipulatives to aid in mathematical thinking. Teachers should ensure 
that the purpose of the chosen technology is to motivate students to think for 
themselves, see patterns, make connections, and gain deep understanding. The 
focus of using technology should not be on increasing the speed of calculations 
and procedures, or making busy work.

Mathematics Curriculum 3.25
ᐊᑐᐃᓐᓇᐅᓂᖏᑦ ᐃᓛᒃᑰᖅᓯᙱᓐᓂᕐᓗ
ATUINNAUNINGIT ILAAKKUUQSINNGINNIRLU
ACCESSIBILITY AND INCLUSION
Each year of the Nunavut Mathematics Curriculum builds on the outcomes 
from the previous year. It is crucial that new learning builds on previously 
acquired knowledge and that allowances are made for frequent review and 
practise. Learners should encounter mathematical experiences that proceed 
from the simple to the complex and from the concrete to the abstract to 
produce meaningful and lasting understanding. 
Students may arrive with varying degrees of understanding of Mathematics. 
Teachers must be aware of these inequities and plan to build foundational 
knowledge for all students. Teachers should ensure that the individual needs 
of each student are considered. There will be a wide range of abilities and 
learning preferences in each classroom, and teachers will therefore have to 
consider students' readiness when planning, teaching, and assessing. The 
curriculum is designed to be flexible in its application, and teachers should 
consider how they can connect outcomes to local contexts and students’ 
interests. Hands-on materials and experiences should be used consistently to 
give students meaningful engagement with the learning.
It is also important to consider local traditional knowledge, practices, and 
language and how they factor into an understanding of Mathematics. 
Elders and other knowledge holders should be invited into the Mathematics 
classroom to share traditional practices and stories and show students how 
Mathematics relates to their lives. As well, educators should be mindful 
of the vast amount of new terminology that is learned when studying 
Mathematics. Students should be encouraged to express their understanding 
in the language of their choice and be given opportunities to count and 
explore numbers in a variety of languages. Educators should focus on the 
concept and application (mathematical skills and processes) as opposed to 
the memorization of terms (language knowledge). The inherent learning and 
assessment should always be on the seeing and doing of Mathematics.

KINDERGARTEN: NUMBER

1. Number 
sense
K.N.1.1 
Say the count sequences from 0 to 20.
 
Notice if the student:
• Says the number names from 1 to 20 (verbal counting).
• Counts backwards from 20 to 1.
• Says the number that comes before or after a given number up to 20.
Additional notes:
Verbal counting means to say the standard list of counting words in order.
Counting should be reinforced through daily school activities.
Students should begin to understand that the next number in a counting sequence 
is always “one more.” 
Students should have easy access to manipulatives and resources to help them (for 
example, number lines, counters, and ten frames) and be encouraged to use them.
K.N.1.2
Represent amounts from 0 to 20 in a variety of ways.
 
Notice if the student:
• Recognizes and records whole numbers to 20 (for example, 1, 2, 3).
• Can show numbers to 20 using manipulatives or concrete objects in their daily life.
• Uses a written numeral to represent or record the total amount in a set of no 
 more than 20 objects.
• Composes and decomposes numbers less than or equal to 20 in a variety of ways.
• Matches numerals to 20 with models or pictures.
• Identifies an empty set and represents it using the numeral 0.

4.2
Additional notes:
Students may need assistance and reminders as they learn to write numerals 
correctly. Ensure that students correctly distinguish between numbers that might 
look similar (for example, 5 and 3).
It is important that students learn that there are many ways to represent an 
amount. Representing numbers is an important concept as it helps students 
develop an understanding of number size as well as relationships to other numbers.
A whole number is a number that does not contain fractional parts or decimals. 
0 is a whole number.
Manipulatives are objects and tools that can be held and moved to show or assist 
in solving math concepts. Examples include ten frames, hundred charts, base-ten 
blocks, coins, counters, fingers, rocks, and linking cubes.
Concrete objects are physical resources that students can handle and manipulate 
to aid their understanding of different math concepts. Examples may include 
counters, rocks, and base-ten blocks. 
A set is a collection of objects or items.
Concrete objects are physical resources that students can handle and manipulate 
to aid their understanding of different math concepts. Examples may include 
counters, rocks, and base-ten blocks.
To compose is to combine numbers to create a larger number.
To decompose is to break down a number into smaller numbers.

Kindergarten ◊ Number
 

Mathematics Curriculum 4.3
K.N.1.3
Compare and order whole numbers from 0 to 20.
 
Notice if the student:
• Uses a variety of strategies such as observing, counting, or matching to determine 
 if one set is greater, less, or the same as another set.
• Determines which of two numbers, written as numerals, is greater or less without 
 counting.
• Writes consecutive whole numbers to 20 in correct sequence.
• Identifies a missing number in a set.
• Orders sets of objects by amount.
• Locates numbers to 20 on a number line.
Additional notes:
A whole number is a number that does not contain fractional parts or decimals. 
A set is a collection of objects or items.
A numeral is the symbol used to represent a number. It is also known as a digit. 
For example, 4 is the numeral for four.
One-to-one correspondence means to count one, and only one object as you say 
the number. For example, touching a counter and moving it to a pile as you count.
Students should match each item from one set with an item from the other set to 
determine which set has more, which set has less, or if both sets have the same.
Students should be encouraged to justify their answers.
The concept of less usually proves more difficult for students to grasp than the 
concept of more. Teachers are encouraged to often ask questions pertaining to 
“which has less” and “which has more.” Many opportunities should be provided for 
students to compare quantities. 

4.4
K.N.1.4
Demonstrate an understanding of cardinality in a counting 
sequence to 20.
 
Notice if the student:
• Moves from left to right when counting up on a number line.
• Points to, separates, or moves objects using one-to-one correspondence when 
 counting.
• Counts to determine the number of objects in a set or group.
• Recognizes that the last number said represents the total number in a countable 
 set.
• Pairs each object with only one number name when counting.
Additional notes:
Cardinality is the concept that the last count word indicates the number of objects 
in the set. For example, when counting three objects, the student knows that when 
they have counted the last object they have their total, which is 3.
One-to-one correspondence means to count one and only one object as you say 
the number. For example, touching a counter and moving it to a pile as you count.
Students should be encouraged to move, touch, or point to objects as they say 
number words. Teachers should model counting strategies for how to keep track of 
what has already been counted (for example, placing the counter on a number line).
A set is a collection of objects or items.
Kindergarten ◊ Number

Mathematics Curriculum 4.5
K.N.1.5
Subitize quantities of 0 to 10 objects.
 
Notice if the student:
• Can say the number of objects in small groups without counting one-by-one.
• Recognizes quantities in patterned sets arranged in a variety of ways (for example, 
 5 on a ten frame).
• Recognizes small groups of objects within larger groups without counting.
• Estimates quantities using a known set and counts to verify estimates.
Additional notes:
To subitize is to tell, at a glance, and without counting, the number of objects or 
items in a small group.
Subitizing is an important skill that plays a major role in students’ early 
understanding of number sense. It is usually best to begin with small quantities 
arranged in different ways. Choose simple objects or shapes (for example, circles or 
squares) and avoid distracting materials for subitizing. Patterns used for subitizing 
should be arranged in a compact manner and be well organized.
Estimate means to find an approximate answer or a value close to the right 
answer.
A set is a collection of objects or items.
2. Operations K.N.2.1
Add and subtract whole numbers with answers to 20 using 
concrete objects.
 
Notice if the student:
• Models addition and subtraction problems to 20 using simple joining and 
 separating activities. 
• Begins to use modelled counting strategies to identify the total number or 
 amount in two or more combined groups to 20.
• Begins to use modelled addition and subtraction strategies to solve problems in 
 daily life.

4.6
Additional notes:
A whole number is a number that does not contain fractional parts or decimals. 
Concrete objects are physical resources that students can handle and manipulate 
to aid their understanding of different math concepts. Examples may include 
counters, rocks, and base-ten blocks. 
Students should be encouraged to observe, model, and participate in class 
discussions about counting two groups of objects.
Part-part-whole representations show the relationship between a whole and 
its parts and refer to the idea that a number can be split or partitioned into 
component parts. Identifying part-part-whole representations is a major milestone 
in early number sense. 
Counting strategies in Kindergarten may include actions such as saying the 
numbers out loud, physically moving the items onto a number line as they count, 
touching or pointing as they count, and recounting to check their answer. 
K.N.2.2
Apply modelled strategies of operation when solving problems.
 
Notice if the student:
• Represents addition and subtraction problems with manipulatives, objects, 
 fingers, drawings, role playing, or verbal explanations.
• Solves addition and subtraction problems using objects, manipulatives, or 
 symbolic representations, with support.
Additional notes:
Manipulatives are objects and tools that can be held and moved to show or assist 
in solving math concepts. Examples include ten frames, hundred charts, base-ten 
blocks, coins, counters, and linking cubes.
At this level, modelled strategies could include activities that involve students 
putting items together or taking them apart. Teachers can model through 
strategies such as thinking out loud, repeated opportunities and routine tasks, and 
having students move counters and speak along with them.
Kindergarten ◊ Number

Mathematics Curriculum 4.7
K.N.2.3
Describe numbers using benchmarks of 5 and 10.
 
Notice if the student:
• Relates numbers to 10 based on their proximity to 5 or 10, with support (for 
 example, 6 is 1 more than 5, and 9 is 1 less than 10). 
• Determines if a given number is closer to 5 or 10 (for example, is 7 closer to 5 or 10?).
• Describes the numbers 5 and 10 in terms of parts (for example, 10 is 2 groups of 5).
Additional notes:
Benchmarks are natural stopping points when counting or comparing numbers 
(for example, 7 is 5 and 2 more). Benchmark numbers 5 and 10 help support 
students’ thinking about the various combinations of other numbers. These 
numbers are the strongest benchmarks in the early grades.
Part-part-whole relationships show the relationship between a whole and 
its parts and refer to the idea that a number can be split or partitioned into 
component parts. Identifying part-part-whole relationships is a major milestone in 
early number sense.
Five frames and ten frames are ideal models for exploring relationships with 5 and 
10 as benchmark numbers. Other models include the fingers on one or two hands.

KINDERGARTEN: PATTERNS AND RELATIONS
Guiding Question: What patterns do you see around you?
Learning 
Concept 
Students will 
learn about:
Learning Outcome
By the end of Kindergarten, students will:
1. Patterns K.PR.1.1
Sort familiar objects into groups based on a single attribute.
 
Notice if the student:
• Recognizes attributes of objects (for example, size and colour).
• Compares objects based on their attributes.
• Identifies the common attribute in a pre-sorted group of objects.
• Begins to participate in discussions about sorting rules.
Additional notes:
An attribute is a characteristic of an object, sound, or action, such as its shape, 
colour, or size.
• Attributes of shapes and objects: faces, vertices, edges, and angles
• Attributes of patterns: colours, direction, shape, and size
A sorting rule is a specific way to arrange or group objects based on their attributes.
Sorting activities help students understand and organize their surroundings. Sorting 
helps students realize that objects can be grouped in different ways based on 
attributes.
Connections can be made between this outcome and outcome K.SS.2.1 in the 
Kindergarten Shape and Space strand.
Kindergarten ◊ Patterns and Relations

Mathematics Curriculum 4.9
K.PR.1.2
Identify and describe simple repeating patterns (e.g., AB, AAB, 
ABB) using everyday objects such as stones and shells. 
 
Notice if the student:
• Shows repeating patterns in sounds, movements, objects, and routines.
• Reads repeating patterns. 
Additional notes:
A repeating pattern is a pattern in which a group of elements repeats itself as the 
pattern extends or continues. For example, ABABAB.
Students should be able to distinguish between examples and non-examples of 
repeating patterns. Reading a pattern involves naming the elements that make up 
the pattern (for example, red-blue-red-blue-red-blue).
Repeating patterns can be found in many different classroom activities and topics. 
These include, but are not limited to, stories, songs, dances, chants, games, nature, 
clothing, animals, calendars, rhymes, seasonal and daily cycles, and frieze patterns.
In Kindergarten, students should be expected to read AB and ABC patterns.
The core is the shortest part of the pattern that repeats.
An AB pattern is a pattern with two distinct elements in its core that repeat. (for 
example, blue, red, blue, red, blue, red).
An ABC pattern is a pattern with three distinct elements in its core that repeat (for 
example, blue, red, green, blue, red, green, blue, red, green).
K.PR.1.3
Represent and extend patterns through various mediums, such 
as drawing, arranging objects, and using digital tools.
 
Notice if the student:
• Copies a repeating pattern that is modelled by the teacher.
• Extends a given repeating pattern in at least one direction.
• Fills in the missing elements of a given repeating pattern.

4.10
Additional notes:
In this grade, students should work with patterns containing two or three elements.
An attribute is a characteristic of an object, sound, or action, such as its shape, 
colour, or size.
• Attributes of patterns: colours, direction, shape, and size
A repeating pattern is a pattern in which a group of elements repeats itself as the 
pattern extends or continues. For example, ABABAB.
Elements are the parts of a pattern—the actual objects, sounds, actions, or items 
used in a pattern, such as squares, cubes, or claps. 
It is important to focus on patterns built on a single attribute, such as colour, shape, 
or size. When modelling repeating patterns, ensure that the pattern core is repeated 
at least three times.
The core is the shortest part of a pattern that repeats.

4.12 Kindergarten ◊ Shape and Space
KINDERGARTEN: SHAPE AND SPACE
Guiding Question: How can we describe the objects around us?
Learning 
Concept 
Students will 
learn about:
Learning Outcome
By the end of Kindergarten, students will:
1. Measurement K.SS.1.1
Identify and describe measurable attributes of lines, 2D, and 3D 
objects.
 
Notice if the student:
• Examines sides, corners, edges, and faces on 2D and 3D objects (for example, 
notices spots that are pointy as corners, notices which 2D and 3D objects have many 
sides and which have none).
• Uses descriptive words to describe objects (for example, “The corners are pointy”; 
 “There are lots of sides”).
Additional notes:
An attribute is a characteristic of an object, sound, or action, such as shape, colour, 
or size.
• Attributes of shapes and objects: faces, vertices, edges, and angles
Measurable attributes are the characteristics of a shape that can be measured, 
such as length, mass, and capacity.
Length refers to the measurement of something from end to end.
Mass refers to how much of something there is.
Capacity refers to the amount that something can hold.
Students should examine the edges, corners, and faces of 3D objects, but are not 
expected to use these terms at this grade level.

Mathematics Curriculum 4.13
K.SS.1.2
Understand and compare sizes, lengths, weights, and capacities 
using everyday objects like stones and shells.
 
Notice if the student:
• Directly compares two objects by placing one beside the other to determine which 
 is longer, shorter, taller, or wider.
• Determines which of two objects is heavier or lighter (for example, holding one in 
 each hand or using a pan balance scale).
• Demonstrates which of two containers holds more or less (for example, filling one 
 container and then pouring the contents into the other to see if it overflows).
Additional notes:
Length refers to the measurement of something from end to end. Length also includes 
height and width at this level.
Mass refers to how much of something there is.
Capacity refers to the amount that something can hold.
Teachers should model using accurate measurement language (for example, saying one 
piece of string is longer, not bigger, than another).
Students should be comparing objects directly (for example beside one another). Note 
the importance of aligning endpoints when comparing length.
K.SS.1.3
Sequence the days of the week and the occurrence of daily events.
 
Notice if the student:
• Says the days of the week in order.
• Connects specific days to familiar events.
• Identifies the day that comes before or after a given day.
• Sequences a set of daily events with support using visuals, actions, or terms such as 
first, next, and last.
Additional notes:
Students can be introduced to a calendar as a visual representation of the days of
the week. Activities could include organizing pictures of daily events to tell a story.
Significant times of day could include school starting, recess time, bedtime, and
wake-up time.
A set is a collection of objects or items. 

4.14 Kindergarten ◊ Shape and Space
K.SS.1.4
Identify Canadian coins and count up to 10 using play money.
 
Notice if the student:
• Identifies Canadian coins by their colours and sizes.
• Recognizes the names of coins like penny, nickel, dime, and quarter.
• Counts a small number of coins (up to 10) using play money.
• Understands basic concepts of value, such as knowing that a dime is worth more 
than a nickel.
• Communicates basic ideas about money and counting using simple language.
Additional notes:
Use colourful and tactile materials like play money and coin replicas to make 
learning engaging and hands-on.
Incorporate storytelling or songs that mention coins to help children remember 
their names and values. Encourage exploration and play with coins in supervised 
settings to build familiarity and confidence with money concepts.
2. 2D shapes and 
3D objects
K.SS.2.1
Identify and name basic 2D shapes (circle, square, triangle, 
rectangle) using everyday objects and natural items.
 
Notice if the student:
• Identifies and names basic 2D shapes (circle, square, triangle, rectangle) 
accurately.
• Uses everyday objects and natural items to demonstrate understanding of 
shapes.
• Describes attributes (e.g., number of sides, angles) of basic 2D shapes correctly.
• Classifies objects based on their shapes with confidence.
• Applies knowledge of shapes to solve simple spatial problems.
Additional notes:
• Descriptions of 2D objects may include language such as: “Circle has no corners”; 
“Triangle has three pointy corners”; “Square has four sides.”

Mathematics Curriculum 4.15
K.SS.2.2
Sort familiar objects using a single shape attribute and explain 
the sorting rule.
 
Notice if the student:
• Explains the rule used to sort a pre-sorted set of 3D objects.
• Arranges 3D objects into groups based on a single attribute provided by the 
teacher.
Additional notes:
An attribute is a characteristic of an object, sound, or action, such as its shape,
colour, or size.
• Attributes of shapes and objects: faces, vertices, edges, and angles
A sorting rule is a specific way to arrange or group objects based on their
attributes.
A set is a collection of objects or items.
Early sorting activities could involve students grouping objects in categories such 
as
things that roll, things that slide, and things that have flat faces.
Students are not expected to know all of the correct geometric names of the 3D
objects at this grade level.
K.SS.2.3
Build and describe 3D objects.
 
Notice if the student:
• Builds a model of a given 3D object.
• Combines and separates 3D objects to make bigger or smaller objects.
• Describes 3D objects using descriptive language.
Additional notes:
Students can use materials such as playdough or modelling clay to build models of 
3D objects.
Students should examine the edges, corners, and faces of 3D objects, but are not 
expected to use these terms at this grade level.
Descriptions of 3D objects may include language such as: “This side is round”; “This 
part is pointy”; “This one reminds me of a juice box.”

4.16 Kindergarten ◊ Shape and Space
3. Transformations K.SS.3.1
Begin to describe location using positional and directional 
language. 
 
Notice if the student:
• Begins to describe the location or position of objects. 
• Locates an object based on an oral description of position or location, with 
 support (for example, the book is under the table).
• Begins to explore direction through movement (for example, dance, sport, games, 
 activities).
Additional notes:
Positional language may include over, under, up, beside, behind, and on top of.
Directional language may include go straight, move in a circle, and look up.
Students can practice transformations using barrier games (see the appendix and 
the Mathematics Educator Handbook for more information).
K.SS.3.2
Predict what transformations 2D and 3D objects can achieve.
 
Notice if the student:
• Predicts and describes transformations (translation, rotation, reflection) that 2D 
and 3D objects can undergo.
• Applies understanding of transformations to predict changes in shape, position, 
and orientation.
• Uses appropriate terminology (e.g., slide, turn, flip) to communicate 
transformations accurately.
• Demonstrates the ability to visualize and manipulate objects mentally through 
transformations.
• Analyzes how transformations affect the symmetry and orientation of objects.
Additional notes
Engage students in hands-on activities where they rearrange classroom furniture 
or organize classroom supplies using transformation concepts.
Encourage students to create floor plans or diagrams to plan and execute 
transformations in practical scenarios.
Integrate discussions on ergonomic design and space optimization using 
transformation strategies.
Foster collaboration among students to solve real-life organizational challenges 
using transformation concepts.
Explore how cultural practices or traditional Inuit methods involve spatial 
organization and transformation techniques.

Mathematics Curriculum 4.17
KINDERGARTEN: STATISTICS AND PROBABILITY
Guiding Question: How can we sort things in our environment?
Learning 
Concept 
Students will 
learn about:
Learning Outcome
By the end of Kindergarten, students will:
 1. Data analysis K.SP.1.1
Begin to ask simple questions about data sets.
 
Notice if the student:
• Begins to formulate and ask simple questions with no more than two possible 
 answers (for example, “Do you like running?”).
• Begins to collect data by gathering responses to questions (for example, adding a 
 counter to the “yes” jar when someone says they like running or the “no” jar when 
 they say they don’t like running).
Additional notes:
A data set is the information that has been collected.
Students ask questions on topics that are familiar and of interest to themselves, 
their classmates, and their environment. Students should use primary first-hand 
data only at this grade level.
First-hand data is information that is collected by oneself or a group of people. 
The person or people are involved in both the creation of questions and collection 
of data.
K.SP.1.2
Collect simple data through observations and activities, such 
as counting objects found in nature, like stones or flowers, 
fostering data collection skills.
 
Notice if the student:
• Sorts sets based on a single attribute and explains the sorting rule (for example, 
 these ones are red and these ones are blue).
• Participates in discussions about sorting data sets to determine the rule for how 
 they were sorted (for example, discusses how the red objects are in one pile and 
 the blue objects are in another pile).

4.18 Kindergarten ◊ Statistics and Probability
Additional notes:
A set is a collection of objects or items.
A data set is the information that has been collected.
An attribute is a characteristic of an object, sound, or action, such as its shape, 
colour, or size.
• Attributes of shapes and objects: faces, vertices, edges, and angles
A sorting rule is a specific way to arrange or group objects based on their attributes.
Students should begin by exploring data about themselves and their environment 
(for example, their favourite things, their classroom, and their playground).
K.SP.1.3
Represent data sets in concrete graphs.
 
Notice if the student:
• Organizes collected data using concrete graphs with one-to-one correspondence 
 in whole-class activities.
Additional notes:
A set is a collection of objects or items.
A data set is the information that has been collected.
One-to-one correspondence means to count one and only one object as you say 
the number (for example, touching a counter and moving it to a pile as you count).
A concrete graph is a graph that records, organizes, and displays data using 
tangible hands-on objects. When using objects to create concrete graphs, it is best to 
arrange each object in a grid, and it is important that the concrete objects used be 
the same shape and size.

Interpret data in concrete graphs.
