• Understands the concept of adding money values and applies it to calculate 
totals in a shopping scenario.
• Organizes and structures calculations neatly and logically to find the total cost 
efficiently.
• Communicates their process and findings clearly, showing understanding of how 
addition applies to real-life shopping situations.
Additional notes:
Set up scenarios where students can "shop" for items with price tags, using play 
money to calculate total costs.
Design interactive games or activities that involve adding prices of items from a 
shopping list, encouraging students to practice addition skills in a practical context. 
Discuss the importance of budgeting and calculating costs when shopping, linking 
these skills to responsible financial decision-making.
Incorporate examples from Inuit communities where similar calculations might 
be necessary, such as budgeting for traditional supplies or planning for communal 
events, to make the learning relevant and culturally sensitive.

4.90 Grade 3 ◊ Shape and Space
2. 2D shapes 
and 3D objects
3.SS.2.1
Identify and describe 3D objects according to the shapes of the 
faces, edges, and vertices.
 
Notice if the student:
• Identifies the edges, faces, and vertices of common 3D objects, including cube, 
 cone, sphere, cylinder, and pyramid.
• Names and describes the shape of the faces of 3D objects.
• Sorts 3D objects based on the number of edges, faces, or vertices .
Additional notes:
Vertex (vertices plural) is the common endpoint where three or more edges meet. 
Also commonly called a corner.
A cube is a 3D object with six square faces, 12 equal straight edges, and eight vertices.
A cuboid is a 3D object with six faces (four or six rectangular faces), 12 straight edges, 
and eight vertices.
A cone is a 3D object with one circular face, one curved edge, one curved surface, and 
one apex (the top of the cone, opposite the base).
A sphere is a 3D object with one curved surface.
A cylinder is a 3D object with two circular faces, one curved surface, and two curved 
edges.
A pyramid is a solid 3D figure that has one base and at least three triangular sides.
A surface is curved (as in a sphere or cylinder), whereas a face is flat (as in the base 
of a cylinder).

playdough and straws or pipe cleaners.
Concrete objects are physical resources that students can handle and manipulate 
to aid their understanding of different math concepts. Examples may include 
counters, rocks, and base-ten blocks.

GRADE 3: STATISTICS AND PROBABILITY
Guiding Question: What can data show us about our world?

Concept 
Students will 
learn about:
Learning Outcome

1. Data analysis 3.SP.1.1
Collect and record data to answer questions about themselves, 
others, and the land. 
 
Notice if the student:
• Plans a method of data collection.
• Collects first-hand data by carrying out an investigation based on a topic of 
 interest. 
• Records and organizes data collected.
Additional notes:
First-hand data is information that is collected by oneself or a group of people. The 
person or people are involved in both the creation of questions and collection of data.
Data collection methods include surveys, interviews, and observations.
Students are expected to use first-hand data (primary data) only at this grade.
3.SP.1.2
Represent data using one-to-one correspondence in frequency

Notice if the student:
• Documents data in a frequency table. 
• Constructs a bar graph to represent data using one-to-one correspondence.
• Uses a frequency table and bar graph to represent the same data.
• Adds appropriate titles and labels axes.

4.94
Additional notes:
Appropriate labels include titles of axes in a bar graph that explain the meaning of 
the data represented.
One-to-one correspondence means to count one and only one object as you say 
the number (for example, touching a counter and moving it to a pile as you count).
Frequency is the number of times an event occurs.
A frequency table is a table that lists data items and shows the number of times 
each occurs.
A bar graph is a graph that presents data with rectangular bars proportional to the 
values that they represent. The bars can be drawn vertically or horizontally.

Read and interpret frequency tables and bar graphs.

3.SP.1.4

Additional notes:
Interpreting data is the process of making sense of collected information by 
analyzing it to identify patterns, trends, and insights, and drawing conclusions that 
help understand what the data means.
Numerical correspondence means that the number of actual objects is accurately 
represented by the pictures on a graph or table.
First-hand data is information that is collected by oneself or a group of people. 
The person or people are involved in both the creation of questions and collection 
of data. At this grade, students are expected to use first-hand data only.

2. Probability 3.SP.2.1
Describe the likelihood of familiar events occurring using 
probability language. 
 
Notice if the student:
• Uses terms such as never, always, sometimes, and maybe to describe the 
 likelihood of an event occurring.
• Identifies events as certain or impossible.
• Explains their reasoning when categorizing probability (for example, I know that 
 it is impossible for caribou to fly because they do not have wings).
Additional notes:
Probability refers to how likely an event is to happen.
Students are encouraged to discuss probability using their preferred language. 
Exploration of probability should focus on familiar topics in daily life (for example, 
community, activities, and school). Teachers are encouraged to invite Elders and 
other knowledge holders into the classroom to share stories about occurrences on 
the land and in the community.
3.SP.2.2
Represent the likelihood of events on a probability line.
 
Notice if the student:
• Uses life experiences and comparisons to determine the likelihood of events.
• Orders the likelihood of familiar events occurring (for example, representing 
 certain, unlikely, likely, and equally likely events on a probability line).
Additional notes:
Probability refers to how likely an event is to happen.
A probability line is used to compare probabilities and show how they relate to each 
other. The events used at this level should be familiar to students and their daily 
lives (for example, weather and school routines).
Students should use words to label probability lines at this level.

Mathematics Curriculum 4.99
GRADE 4: NUMBER
Guiding Question: How can we represent numbers less than one?
Learning 
Concept 
Students will 
learn about:
Learning Outcome
By the end of Grade 4, students will:
1. Number 
sense
4.N.1.1
Represent whole numbers or amounts from 0 to 100,000 in a 
variety of ways.
 
Notice if the student:
• Recognizes and records whole numbers up to 100,000 (for example, 2500, 6981, 
and 9347).
• Demonstrates numbers up to 100,000 using manipulatives, models, symbols, 
and pictorial representations.
• Uses place-value properties (including 0 as a placeholder) to represent a given 
whole number, up to 100,000, as groups of 10,000s, 1000s, 100s, 10s, and 1s and 1s 
(for example, 7352 is 7 thousands, 3 hundreds, 5 tens, and 2 ones).
• Expresses two-, three-, four-, and five-digit numbers given in standard form in 
expanded form, and vice versa.
• Identifies and explains the place value of the digits in two-, three-, four-, and 
five-digit numbers.
Additional notes:
Manipulatives are objects and tools that can be held and moved to show or 
assist in solving math concepts. Examples include ten frames, hundred charts, 
base-ten blocks, coins, counters, and linking cubes. Students should continue using 
manipulatives such as base-ten blocks to represent numbers.
Standard form refers to the way we normally write numbers (for example, 276 is 
written in standard form).
Using expanded form means to rewrite the digits of a number based on their place 
values (for example, 254 = 200 + 50 + 4).
Place value refers to the value a numeral holds based on its place in a number.
Whole numbers can be separated into place-value groups, such as 1s, 10s, 100s, 1,000s, 
and 1,000,000s. Each of these groups contains three subgroups: 1s, 10s, and 100s. 
When students are reading or writing large numbers, they should begin at the left 
with the largest group and proceed to the right.

4.100
4.N.1.2
Compare and order whole numbers from 0 to 100,000.
 
Notice if the student:
• Uses greater than (>) or less than (<) symbols to compare whole numbers up to 100,000.
• Arranges numbers to 100,000 in ascending or descending order.
• Rounds numbers to the nearest 10 100, 1000, and 10,000.
• Identifies the missing number in a sequence up to 100,000.
Additional notes:
A whole number is a number that does not contain fractional parts or decimals.
Ascending order means to list values from least to greatest.
Descending order means to list values from greatest to least.
Rounding means to replace a number with a simpler number, keeping the value 
close to what it was originally. Numbers are typically rounded to multiples of 5, 10, 
100, 1,000, etc.
Students can use number lines and hundred charts as useful tools when rounding.
4.N.1.3
Demonstrate parts of a whole using decimals to tenths.
 
Notice if the student:
• Uses prior knowledge of fractions to understand decimals to tenths.
• Uses decimals (notation) to describe or represent a part of a whole, part of a set, 
 or part of a region that is divided into 10 equal parts.
• Represents decimals to tenths concretely and pictorially.
• Explains the meaning of the digit in a decimal number.
Additional notes:
A decimal acts as a separation of a whole number from its fractional part.
A fraction is part of a whole. For example, -
1 
4
 of a dollar is a quarter or 25 cents, 
-
5 
10 is half of 10.
A set is a collection of objects or items.
Students should fully understand the concept of fraction tenths before they are 
introduced to decimals.
Concrete and pictorial representations should be used to help students develop the 
understanding that decimals extend the place-value system to represent parts of a 
whole.

A decimal acts as a separation of a whole number from its fractional part.
Comparison of fractions and decimals are only valid if they refer to or represent

The number line is a great tool for comparing fractions and decimals.
The denominator is the number in a fraction that shows the number of equal 
parts that the whole is divided into. It is the bottom number in a fraction.

Ascending order means to list values from least to greatest.

4.N.1.5
Understand and apply place value concepts to read, write, and 
compare multi-digit whole numbers up to 100,000 using baseten numerals, number names, and expanded form. 
 
Notice if the student:
• Reads multi-digit numbers up to 100,000.
• Recognizes the value of each digit in a number and can articulate this verbally 
(e.g., in 453,276, the '4' represents four hundred thousand).
• Writes numbers up to 100,000 correctly in standard form (base-ten numerals), 
word form (number names), and expanded form.
• Demonstrates understanding by converting between these forms accurately.
• Compares multi-digit numbers using comparison symbols (> , < , =).
• Explains the reasoning behind the comparisons, showing an understanding of 
the value of each place.
• Demonstrates understanding of place value through decomposing numbers into 
their constituent parts (e.g., 789,456 is 700,000 + 80,000 + 9,000 + 400 + 50 + 6).
• Applies place value knowledge to solve related problems, such as addition and 
subtraction of multi-digit numbers.
Additional notes:
A whole number is a number that does not contain fractional parts or decimals.
A number sentence is a mathematical equation that is made up of numbers and 
signs indicating an equality or inequality (for example, 23 + 76 = 99). In a number 
sentence, a number that is added to another number is called an addend, and a 
number that is going to be subtracted is called the subtrahend.
Using expanded form means to rewrite the digits of a number based on their 
place values (for example, 254 = 200 + 50 + 4).
Rounding means to replace a number with a simpler number, keeping the value 
close to what it was originally. Numbers are typically rounded to multiples of 5, 10, 
100, 1,000, etc.
Grade 4 ◊ Number

Mathematics Curriculum 4.103
2. Operations 4.N.2.1
Add and subtract whole numbers with answers up to 10,000, 
using a variety of strategies.
 
Notice if the student:
• Adds and subtracts whole numbers with answers up to 10,000 with and without 
 regrouping.
• Determines if number sentences are true or false.
• Solves addition problems involving the commutative and associative properties.
• Chooses efficient methods when solving addition and subtraction problems.
• Estimates sums and differences to 10,000.
Additional notes:
A whole number is a number that does not contain fractional parts or decimals.
A number sentence is a mathematical equation that is made up of numbers and 
signs indicating an equality or inequality (for example, 23 + 76 = 99). In a number 
sentence, a number that is added to another number is called an addend, and a 
number that is going to be subtracted is called the subtrahend.
Regrouping means to combine objects into groups of 10 to make adding and 
subtracting simpler.
The associative property of addition is the idea that, when we add three or 
more numbers, the sum is the same regardless of the order in which we add the 
numbers.
Commutative property of addition is the idea that the order in which we add a 
set of numbers does not change the result (for example, 4 + 2 and 2 + 4 both equal 6).
To estimate means to find an approximate answer or a value close to the right 
answer. 
4.N.2.2
Add and subtract decimal numbers to tenths.
 
Notice if the student:
• Uses a variety of strategies, including place-value concepts and lining up decimal 
 points in vertical addition, to solve problems involving decimals.
• Determines if number sentences involving decimals are true or false.

A decimal acts as a separation of a whole number from its fractional part.

operation when solving problems.
Strategies may include rounding, front-end addition and subtraction, and vertical 
addition.
Front-end addition means adding numbers of two or more digits by starting 
with the digits of the largest place value and then adding the remaining sums. For 
example:
Vertical addition means to place numbers on top of each other, and working 
from right to left. This method can involve carrying and borrowing amounts. For 
example:

signs indicating an equality or inequality (for example, 23 + 76 = 99).

4.N.2.4
Demonstrate mental math strategies to determine 
multiplication and related division facts to 100.
 
Notice if the student:
• Recalls multiplication facts for 2s, 5s, and 10s to 100.
• Begins to recall some multiplication and related division facts to 10 x 10.
• Uses mental math strategies, including skip counting, repeated addition, or 
 doubling, to multiply and divide whole numbers.
• Multiplies whole numbers by 10 and 100.
• Explains the effect of multiplying or dividing a whole number by 1 or by 0.
Additional notes:
Mental math means to do calculations in one’s head using a variety of strategies to 
solve a problem. Strategies may include recalling doubles and near doubles, making 
10, doubles plus one, and thinking addition to subtract. See the Mathematics 
Educator Handbook for more information on mental math strategies. 
Additional mental strategies for multiplication and division include using 
multiplication to divide, doubling or halving, and using patterns in the nine times 
table. 
Skip counting means to count up repeatedly using the same number (for example, 
10, 20, 30, 40, etc.
Repeated addition means to add the same number repeatedly (for example, 6 + 6 = 
12, 12 + 6 = 18, etc.
Doubling means to add a number to itself to double the amount (for example, 100 + 
100 = 200).
A whole number is a number that does not contain fractional parts or decimals.

4.106
4.N.2.5
Represent and solve multiplication problems that involve 
multiplying two- and three-digit numbers by one-digit 
numbers, using a variety of strategies.
 
Notice if the student:
• Demonstrates multiplication problems using hands-on materials (for example, 
 using base-ten blocks and arrays).
• Applies the commutative, associative, and zero properties of multiplication.
• Multiplies whole numbers by 10 using place-value concepts.
Additional notes:
Multiplication strategies may include using repeated addition, arrays, area models, 
standard algorithms, and multiplication tables. 
At this grade level students should only be working with multiplication problems 
without regrouping.
The commutative property of multiplication is the idea that the order in which 
we multiply a set of numbers does not change the result.
The associative property of multiplication is the idea that, if we have three or 
more numbers to multiply, we can multiply them in any grouping that we want, 
and this does not change the result.
The zero property of multiplication is the idea that whenever a number is 
multiplied by 0, the product is always 0.
Repeated addition means to add the same number repeatedly (for example, 6 + 6 = 
12, 12 + 6 = 18, etc.)
Multiplication tables show the mathematical operations of multiplying numbers 
from 0 to 12, which can be displayed on a table.
A whole number is a number that does not contain fractional parts or decimals.
Grade 4 ◊ Number

Mathematics Curriculum 4.107
4.N.2.6
Represent and solve division problems that involve dividing 
one- and two-digit numbers by one-digit numbers, with or 
without remainder. 
 
Notice if the student:
• Demonstrates division as equal sharing and repeated subtraction.
• Divides a one- or two-digit number by a one-digit number with or without a 
remainder.
Additional notes:
Division problems should provide opportunities for students to use concrete 
models and a variety of strategies, including concrete and pictorial representations, 
estimation, and standard algorithms.
Division facts are division number sentences related to students’ multiplication 
tables knowledge. For example, 30 ÷ 5 = 6, 15 ÷ 5 = 3, and 20 ÷ 5 = 4 are all division 
facts of the 5 times table.
Repeated subtraction means to subtract the same number from a larger number 
each time (for example, 36 – 6 = 30, 30 – 6 = 24, etc.)
Multiplication tables show the mathematical operations of multiplying numbers 
from 0 to 12, which can be displayed on a table.

 and tables.

• Identifies and corrects errors in a chart or table.

Additional notes:
The pattern rule refers to how each element in a pattern behaves and the 
relationships among the elements in a pattern.
An increasing pattern is a pattern that gets bigger in the same way repeatedly.
A decreasing pattern is a pattern that gets smaller in the same way repeatedly.

An increasing pattern is a pattern that gets bigger in the same way repeatedly.
A decreasing pattern is a pattern that gets smaller in the same way repeatedly.
A pattern rule refers to how each element in a pattern behaves and the 
relationships among the elements in a pattern.

GRADE 4: SHAPE AND SPACE
Guiding Question: How can we measure spaces and shapes?
Learning 
Concept 
Students will 
learn about:
Learning Outcome
By the end of Grade 4, students will:
1. Measurement 4.SS.1.1
Demonstrate the relationships between millimetres, 
centimetres, metres, and kilometres.
 
Notice if the student:
• Considers why we use a variety of units of measurement, and when each is 
 appropriate.
• Converts units of measurement (for example, converts millimetres to centimetres 
 or metres to kilometres).
Additional notes:
There are 10 millimetres in 1 centimetre.
There are 1,000 millimetres in 1 metre.
There are 100 centimetres in 1 metre.
There are 1,000 metres in 1 kilometre
Students should use concrete materials to demonstrate these equivalencies.
4.SS.1.2
Solve problems involving the perimeter of regular and irregular 
polygons, including finding an unknown side.
 
Notice if the student:
• Uses addition to find the perimeter.
• Uses prior knowledge of perimeter to solve the perimeter of irregular polygons.
• Calculates the length of an unknown side of a polygon or object given the 
 perimeter and the length of the other sides. 
Additional notes:
Perimeter is the distance around a shape along its border.
A polygon is a closed shape with at least three sides and three angles.
A regular polygon is a 2D shape that has all equal sides and angles (for example, 
an equilateral triangle, a square, or a regular hexagon).
Grade 4 ◊ Shape and Space

Mathematics Curriculum 4.113
An irregular polygon is a 2D shape with sides and angles that are not all equal (for 
example, a rectangle).
In this grade, objects or polygons should be limited to those with no more than four 
sides.
Length refers to the measurement of something from end to end.
Sides can be marked off with unit lengths or labelled with numbers.
4.SS.1.3
Estimate and measure area using non-standard and square units.
 
Notice if the student:
• Compares the area of two shapes by using one shape to cover the other.
• Covers shapes with non-standard units to determine which has the larger area.
• Makes reasonable estimates of areas of shapes using non-standard units.
• Determines the best non-standard unit to cover the area of a shape and justifies 
 their choice.
Additional notes:
To estimate means to find an approximate answer or a value close to the right 
answer. 
Area is the amount of space taken up by a 2D figure. Area is measured in square 
units (for example, square millimetres, square centimetres, square metres, etc)
Students should initially begin by measuring areas with units that do not fit closely 
together (for example, using hexagons on a square area) in order to help them 
deduce that the square unit is best.
Non-standard units are items used to measure something approximately, without 
referring to a standard common unit of measurement, such as centimetres. Nonstandard units may be uniform (always the same size) or non-uniform (differing in 
size). Uniform units may include interlocking cubes, base-ten blocks, and pattern 
blocks. Non-uniform units may include hands, feet, and pencils.
It is important that students experiment with measuring the same area using a 
variety of non-standard units (for example, measuring the area of a desk with a 
book versus square tiles).
A square unit is equal to the area of a square with a side length of 1 unit.

4.118 Grade 4 ◊ Statistics and Probability
GRADE 4: STATISTICS AND PROBABILITY
Guiding Question: Why do we use different methods to represent data?
Learning 
Concept 
Students will 
learn about:
Learning Outcome
By the end of Grade 4, students will:
1. Data analysis 4.SP.1.1
Collect and organize larger data sets through experiments and 
research, demonstrating proficiency in managing and organizing 
data effectively.
 
Notice if the student:
• Formulates and asks questions to gather data.
• Collects first-hand data using surveys, interviews, and observations.
• Creates recording sheets to organize data collected (for example, tally charts).
Additional notes:
First-hand data is information that is collected by oneself or a group of people. The 
person or people are involved in both the creation of questions and the collection of 
data. 
A survey is a method of collecting data by asking questions.
A tally chart is a table with tally marks to show values in a data set.
Students are expected to use first-hand (primary) data only at this grade level.
4.SP.1.2
Use multiple types of graphs and tables to represent data, 
including histograms, developing skills in selecting appropriate 
representations for different types of data.
 
Notice if the student:
• Uses prior knowledge of one-to-one correspondence to use many-to-one-
 correspondence.
• Constructs pictographs, bar graphs, and frequency tables in which data is 
 displayed using many-to-one correspondence (for example, more than one object 
 may be represented as a group, such as one star in a pictograph representing 
 five stars).
• Chooses an appropriate legend to represent data.
• Creates appropriate titles and labels axes.
• Accurately charts data on pictographs, frequency tables, and bar graphs.

Mathematics Curriculum 4.119
Additional notes:
Many-to-one correspondence refers to representing many objects by one object or 
interval on a graph (for example, in a pictograph, 1 smiley face can represent 5 students).
A legend is a tool that helps explain the values in a graph. It shows the ways the 
numbers or pictures are used to represent data. It is also known as a scale.
As students work with larger amounts of data, it becomes necessary to use a single 
symbol to represent several items.
A pictograph is a graph that uses pictures or symbols to show the value of data. 
One-to-one correspondence means to count one and only one object as you say 
the number (for example, touching a counter and moving it to a pile as you count).
Frequency is the number of times an event occurs.
A frequency table is a table that lists data items and shows the number of times 
each occurs.
A bar graph is a graph that presents data with rectangular bars proportional to the 
values that they represent. The bars can be drawn vertically or horizontally.
4.SP.1.3
Perform more detailed analysis of data, including measures of 
central tendency (mean, median, mode).
 
Notice if the student:
• Accurately calculates measures of central tendency, including mean, median, 
and mode, from given data sets.
• Understands the differences between mean, median, and mode and their 
appropriate uses.
• Interprets data and explains what the measures of central tendency indicate 
about the data set.
• Applies these concepts to real-world data relevant to their experiences and 
community.
• Communicates their findings clearly and uses appropriate terminology.
Additional notes:
Mean is the average of a set of numbers, found by adding all the numbers together 
and dividing by the total number of numbers.
Median is the middle value in a set of numbers arranged in order, or the average 
of the two middle numbers if the set has an even number of values.
Mode is the number that appears most frequently in a set of numbers.

4.120 Grade 4 ◊ Statistics and Probability
Encourage group work where students collect their own data, calculate measures of 
central tendency, and present their findings.
Discuss the significance and practical applications of mean, median, and mode in 
everyday decision-making, such as budgeting or planning community events.
4.SP.1.4
Analyze data from community-based projects, such as 
studying local wildlife populations or traditional hunting 
practices, integrating cultural contexts into data analysis and 
interpretation.
 
Notice if the student:
• Uses data in graphs and tables to answer questions.
• Makes reasonable inferences and predictions based on data in graphs and tables.
• Compares the effectiveness of different data displays.
Additional notes:
Students should be able to provide justification for inferences made from data 
displays.
Students should be able to create and compare different representations of the 
same data.
Many-to-one correspondence refers to representing many objects by one object 
or interval on a graph (for example, in a pictograph, 1 smiley face can represent 5 
students).
Graphs and tables should include many-to-one correspondence.
Frequency is the number of times an event occurs.
A frequency table is a table that lists data items and shows the number of times 
each occurs.
A pictograph is a graph that uses pictures or symbols to show the value of data.
A bar graph is a graph that presents data with rectangular bars proportional to the 
values that they represent. The bars can be drawn vertically or horizontally. 

Mathematics Curriculum 4.121
2. Chance and 
uncertaincy
4.SP.2.1
Compare the likelihood of events occurring in experiments 
using probability language.
 
Notice if the student:
• Identifies and lists all possible outcomes of simple experiments.
• Describes the outcomes of events as likely, unlikely, or equally likely.
• Predicts and tests the likelihood of single-outcome events.
Additional notes
Probability refers to how likely an event is to happen.
Students are encouraged to share probability words in a variety of local languages.
Simple experiments could include tossing a coin, rolling a dice, or spinning a wheel.
Equally likely events have the same probability (for example, if you roll a die, you 
are equally likely to get a 1 or a 6). Less likely events have lower probability (for 
example, when you pull a card from a deck, you are less likely to get a joker than 
any other type of card because there are only 2 jokers in the deck).
4.SP.2.2 
Use charts and graphs to represent the outcomes of probability 
experiments.
 
Notice if the student:
• Uses terms such as less likely, equally likely, and more likely to describe the 
 possibility of an event.
• Participates in probability experiments and games.
Additional notes:
Probability refers to how likely an event is to happen.
Students begin to represent probabilities numerically and connect these to 
probability language used in earlier grades.
Other ways to represent probability may include pictures, spinners, and tally charts.

Mathematics Curriculum 4.123
GRADE 5: NUMBER
Guiding Question: How do we use numbers less than zero?
Learning 
Concept 
Students will 
learn about:
Learning Outcome
By the end of Grade 5, students will:
1. Number sense 5.N.1.1
Represent whole numbers or amounts between 0 and 1,000,000 
in a variety of ways.
 
Notice if the student:
• Recognizes and records whole numbers up to 1,000 000 (for example, 25 167, 50,000, 
 and 99 204).
• Demonstrates numbers up to 1,000,000 using manipulatives, models, symbols, or 
 pictorial representations (for example, coins and bills).
• Uses place-value properties (including zero as a placeholder) to represent a given 
 whole number up to 1,000,000 as groups of 100,000s, 10,000s, 1,000s, 100s, 10s,
 and 1s.
• Expresses up to six-digit numbers given in standard form in expanded form.
• Expresses up to six-digit numbers given in expanded form in standard form.
• Identifies and explains the place value of digits in numbers up to six-digits long.
Additional notes:
A whole number is a number that does not contain fractional parts or decimals.
Manipulatives are objects and tools that can be held and moved to show or assist 
in solving math concepts. Examples include ten frames, hundred charts, base-ten 
blocks, coins, counters, and linking cubes.
Standard form refers to the way we normally write numbers (for example, 276 is 
written in standard form).
Using expanded form means to rewrite the digits of a number based on their place 
values (for example, 254 = 200 + 50 + 4).
Place value refers to the value a numeral holds based on its place in a number.
Whole numbers can be separated into place-value groups, such as 1s, 10s, 100s, 1,000s, 
and 1,000,000s. Each of these groups contains three subgroups: 1s, 10s, and 100s. 
When students are reading or writing large numbers, they should begin at the left 
with the largest group and proceed to the right.

5.N.1.2
Compare and order whole numbers from 0 to 1,000,000.
 
Notice if the student:
• Compares numbers up to 1,000,000 using greater than (>) or less than (<) symbols.
• Arranges numbers up to 1,000,000 in ascending or descending order.
• Rounds numbers to the nearest 10, 100, 1,000, and 10,000, and 100,000.
• Explains patterns and identifies the missing number or numbers, to 1,000,000, in 
 a sequence.
Additional notes:
A whole number is a number that does not contain fractional parts or decimals.
To compare whole numbers with the same number of digits, determine which 
number has the largest digit first (for example, 534 and 623 have the same number 
of digits, but 623 has the largest digit first).
To compare whole numbers that have a different number of digits, look at the place 
values of each number. The number that extends the farthest to the left is the 
larger number. 
Ascending order means to list values from least to greatest.
Descending order means to list values from greatest to least.
Rounding means to replace a number with a simpler number, keeping the value 
close to what it was originally.
Students can use a number line or hundred chart as a useful tool when rounding.
5.N.1.3
Demonstrate parts of a whole using decimals to hundredths.
 
Notice if the student:
• Uses prior knowledge of fractions to understand decimals to hundredths.
• Uses decimals to describe a part of a whole, or part of a set, that is divided into 
 100 equal parts.
• Represents decimals to hundredths concretely and pictorially.
• Explains the meaning of the digits in a decimal number.
• Shows money amounts using decimals (for example, $1 and 25 cents is $1.25, and 
 $10 and 5 cents is $10.05).

A decimal acts as a separation of a whole number from its fractional part.
Decimals less than 1 are usually written with a 0 before the decimal point. 
The decimal point is used to identify the one’s place, and it separates the whole part 
of a number from the fractional part (for example, in 2.3, the 2 is the whole, and the 
3 is the fractional part).

5.N.1.5
Represent equivalent fractions for commonly used fractions.
 
Notice if the student:
• Creates simple equivalent fractions concretely and pictorially.

Additional notes:
A fraction is part of a whole. For example, -
1 
4
 of a dollar is a quarter or 25 cents, 
-
5 
10 is half of 10.
Two fractions are equivalent if both result in the same fraction after being simplified. 
Simplifying a fraction is when we demonstrate a fraction in the simplest way (for 
example, instead of 15/20, we can say 3/4). 
Equivalent fractions are fractions that represent the same location on a number 
line or the same part of the same whole. Every fraction has an infinite number of 
equivalent fractions.
Students can create equivalent fractions using area models and number lines.
5.N.1.6
Understand and use place value to read, write, and compare 
decimals to thousandths, and to round decimals to any given place. 
 
Notice if the student:
• Reads decimal numbers to the thousandths place.
• Understands and articulates the value of each digit in a decimal number (e.g., in 
3.456, the '4' is in the tenths place and represents four-tenths or 4/10).
• Writes decimal numbers correctly in standard form, word form, and expanded form 
(e.g., 3.456 as three and four hundred fifty-six thousandths or 3 + 0.4 + 0.05 + 0.006).
• Compares decimal numbers using comparison symbols (> , < , =).
• Explains the reasoning behind comparisons, demonstrating an understanding of 
place value.
• Accurately rounds decimals to a given place (e.g., rounds 3.456 to the nearest tenth 
as 3.5).
• Demonstrates understanding of rounding rules and applies them consistently.
• Applies place value understanding to solve problems involving decimals, such as 
addition and subtraction.
• Decomposes decimal numbers into their place value components (e.g., 3.456 as 3 + 0.4 
+ 0.05 + 0.006).
Additional notes:
Place holders in a decimal numbers have "-ths" added to the end of them. For 
example, the number 3.456 has 4 in the tenths place holder, 5 in the hundredths place 
holder, and 6 in the thousandths place holder.
A fraction is part of a whole. For example, ¼ of a dollar is a quarter or 25 cents, 5/10 is 
half of 10.
Grade 5 ◊ Number

Mathematics Curriculum 4.127
A decimal acts as a separation of a whole number from its fractional part.
Comparison of fractions and decimals are only valid if they refer to or represent 
parts of the same whole.
The number line is a great tool for comparing fractions and decimals.
The denominator is the number in a fraction that shows the number of equal 
parts that the whole is divided into. It is the bottom number in a fraction.
Ascending order means to list values from least to greatest.
Descending order means to list values from greatest to least.
Rounding means to replace a number with a simpler number, keeping the value 
close to what it was originally. Numbers are typically rounded to multiples of 5, 10, 
100, 1000, ect.
To round a number, take a look at the digit you are rounding and look to the right 
of that digit. If the digit to the right is 5 or greater, round the digit to the left up. If 
it's less than 5, the digit stays the same. For example, with the number 4.825, if you 
are rounding to the nearest tenth, the number becomes 4.8. If you are rounding to 
the nearest hundredth, the number becomes 4.83.
A whole number is a number that does not contain fractional parts or decimals.

Additional notes:
A whole number is a number that does not contain fractional parts or decimals.
A decimal acts as a separation of a whole number from its fractional part.
To estimate means to find an approximate answer or a value close to the right 
answer.

Grade 5 ◊ Number

Mathematics Curriculum 4.129
The commutative property of multiplication is the idea that the order in which 
we multiply a set of numbers does not change the result.
The associative property of multiplication is the idea that, if we have three or 
more numbers to multiply, we can multiply them in any grouping that we want, 
and this does not change the result.
A decimal acts as a separation of a whole number from its fractional part.
A whole number is a number that does not contain fractional parts or decimals.
5.N.2.4
Solve division problems that involve one-, two-, and three-digit 
numbers.

Additional notes:

and multiplication tables. 
Students must understand what the remainder in a division problem means and be 
able to express it symbolically.
The quotient is the number that results when you divide one number by another.
A divisor is a number by which another number is divided in an equation. When 
you divide 21 by 7, 7 is the divisor.
A dividend is the number being divided in an equation. When you divide 21 by 7, 21 
is the dividend.
A fraction is part of a whole. For example, -
1 
4
 of a dollar is a quarter or 25 cents, 
-
5 
10 is half of 10.
Repeated subtraction means to subtract the same number from a larger number 
each time (for example, 36 – 6 = 30, 30 – 6 = 24, etc)
A standard algorithm is a series of steps that is used to solve a mathematical 
problem.
Multiplication tables show the mathematical operations of multiplying numbers 
from 0 to 12, which can be displayed on a table.

An expression is a math statement with at least two different numbers (known or
unknown) and at least one operation.
For example:
x + 3
m - 8
To evaluate an algebraic expression means to find the value of the expression when
the variable is replaced by a given number. To simplify an expression means to
solve the problem or write it in the simplest form.
A variable is a letter or term that represents an unknown number, value, or quantity.

GRADE 5: SHAPE AND SPACE
Guiding Question: What are the relationships between perimeter, area, and angles? 
Learning 
Concept 
Students will 
learn about:
Learning Outcome
By the end of Grade 5, students will:
1. Measurement 5.SS.1.1
Solve problems involving the area of rectangles and squares. 
 
Notice if the student:
• Determines area by counting square units on a grid.
• Applies the formula for calculating the area of squares and rectangles.
• Records area in square units.
• Constructs rectangles of a given area.
• Finds the length of one side of a rectangle when given the length of the other 
 side and the area.
Additional notes:
In Grade 5, students should be working with whole-number side lengths when 
solving problems involving the area of rectangles and squares.
Area is the amount of space taken up by a 2D figure. Area is measured in square 
units (for example, square millimetres, square centimetres, square metres, etc)
The area of a rectangle is equal to its length times its width OR its base length 
times its height.
A square unit is equal to the area of a square with a side length of 1 unit.
Length refers to the measurement of something from end to end.

Area is the amount of space taken up by a 2D figure. Area is measured in square

A referent is an object or known value that can be used to help us estimate a

2. 2D shapes and

Notice if the student:
• Names 3D objects based on their attributes.
• Compares attributes (edges, vertices, and faces) to determine if an object is a 
 prism, pyramid, or cone.
• Sorts prisms, pyramids, and cones based on their attributes.
• Identifies prisms, pyramids, and cones in their environment.
Additional notes:
A prism is a solid 3D figure that has two congruent parallel faces.
A pyramid is a solid 3D figure that has one base and at least three triangular sides.

A cone is a 3D object with one circular face, one curved edge, one curved surface, 
and one apex (the top of the cone, opposite the base).
An attribute is a characteristic of an object, sound, or action, such as its shape, 
colour, or size.
• Attributes of shapes and objects: faces, vertices, edges, and angles
5.SS.2.2

Additional notes: A prism is a solid 3D figure that has two congruent parallel faces.
A pyramid is a solid 3D figure that has one base and at least three triangular sides.
A cone is a 3D object with one circular face, one curved edge, one curved surface, 
and one apex (the top of the cone, opposite the base).
A net is a 2D shape that can be folded to make a 3D object.

3. Transformations 5.SS.3.1
Perform single transformations on 2D shapes. 
 
Notice if the student:
• Demonstrates single transformations on simple 2D shapes and describes what 
 changes and what stays the same.
• Identifies and describes a transformation given the pre-image and image.
Additional notes:
Transformation involves moving an object from its original position to a new position. 
Transformations include reflection (flip), rotation (turn), and translation (slide).
In Grade 5, students are not expected to perform transformations on the coordinate 
plane. They are also not required to know the formal names (reflection, rotation, 
and translation) of the transformations at this level. Transformations that result in 
a change in size are not expected to be taught at this level. 
The image shows the shape after a transformation.
The pre-image shows the shape before a transformation.

Mathematics Curriculum 4.143
GRADE 5: STATISTICS AND PROBABILITY
Guiding Question: How can we ensure data is accurate?
Learning 
Concept 
Students will 
learn about:
Learning Outcome
By the end of Grade 5, students will:
1. Data analysis 5.SP.1.1
Collect and record first- and second-hand data using a variety of 
methods.
 
Notice if the student:
• Asks questions about data sets.
• Identifies sources of second-hand data.
• Collects and compares second-hand data from a variety of sources.
• Begins to distinguish between reliable and unreliable data sources.
• Determines what data is useful and accurate.
Additional notes:
A set is a collection of objects or items.
A data set is the information that has been collected.
In this grade, students should continue working with first-hand data and begin 
working with second-hand data.
First-hand data is information that is collected by oneself or a group of people. 
The person or people are involved in both the creation of questions and collection 
of data.
Second-hand data is information that is collected by someone else. The student is 
not part of creating and answering the question. 
Sources of second-hand data may include government websites, maps, news, sports 
statistics, and reference books. The Internet makes it very easy to find second-hand 
data; however, guided discussions on determining what makes a source reliable are 
very important to teach students how to determine the quality of a source.
Comparing reliable and unreliable data sources connects to the Social Studies 
Curriculum and the Physical Education, Health, and Wellness Curriculum.

4.144
5.SP.1.2
Represent data using one-to-one and many-to-one 
correspondence in double-bar graphs.
 
Notice if the student:
• Constructs double-bar graphs to represent data.
• Creates appropriate titles, scales, and labels for axes.
• Accurately charts data on double-bar graphs using one-to-one and many-to-one 
 correspondence.
• Determines situations where it is valuable to compare two data sets side by side.
Additional notes:
One-to-one correspondence means to count one and only one object as you say 
the number (for example, touching a counter and moving it to a pile as you count).
Many-to-one correspondence refers to representing many objects by one object 
or interval on a graph (for example, in a pictograph, 1 smiley face can represent 5 
students).
A bar graph is a graph that presents data with rectangular bars proportional to the 
values that they represent. The bars can be drawn vertically or horizontally.
A double-bar graph is a graph used to show two sets of data on the same graph.
A properly constructed graph will have a title, labelled axes, and a scale or legend.
A set is a collection of objects or items.
A data set is the information that has been collected. Number of Students

Grade 5 ◊ Statistics and Probability

Mathematics Curriculum 4.145
5.SP.1.3
Read and interpret double-bar graphs.
 
Notice if the student:
• Answers questions based on data in double-bar graphs.
• Uses double-bar graphs to compare two sets of data. 
• Reads and interprets the legend on double-bar graphs.
• Makes reasonable comparisons, inferences, and predictions based on data in 
graphs.
Additional notes:
Students will build on prior knowledge of frequency tables to understand bar graphs. 
A bar graph is a graph that presents data with rectangular bars proportional to the 
values that they represent. The bars can be drawn vertically or horizontally.
A double-bar graph is a graph used to show two sets of data on the same graph.
Students should be able to provide justification for inferences made from data 
displays.
A set is a collection of objects or items.
A data set is the information that has been collected.
A legend is a tool that helps explain the values in a graph. It shows the ways the 
numbers or pictures are used to represent data. 
2. Chance 
and uncertainty
5.SP.2.1
Represent the probabilities of independent events in a variety of 
ways, including fractions and ratios.
 
Notice if the student:
• Describes a probability as a ratio (for example, 3 out of the 7 pencils are yellow, so 
 the probability of picking a yellow pencil is 3:7).
• Represents simple probabilities as fractions.
• Represents probabilities as fractions on a number line.
Additional notes:
Probability refers to how likely an event is to happen.
Two events are independent if the occurrence of one does not influence the other.
The probability of an event is the number of possible ways an outcome can occur 
divided by the total number of outcomes.
A fraction is part of a whole. For example, -
1 
4
 of a dollar is a quarter or 25 cents, 
-
5 
10 is half of 10.
A ratio is a comparison between two quantities.

4.146
5.SP.2.2
Predict and test the probability of an independent event through 
single-outcome experiments.
 
Notice if the student:
• Makes predictions about single-outcome experiments.
• Designs and carries out probability experiments in which one outcome is more 
 likely, less likely, or equally as likely as other outcomes.
• Compares the theoretical likelihood of different events in single-outcome 
 probability experiments.
• Records the results of single-outcome experiments.
Additional notes:
Probability refers to how likely an event is to happen.
Theoretical probability refers to what we expect to happen based on mathematical 
probability.
For example:
• Whether you roll a die 6 times or 60 times, the probability of rolling a 1 is still 1/6
• Theoretically we have a 1 in 6 chance of rolling a 1 on a die, but it is possible our 
 actual results may be more or less than 1/6.
• If we spin two identical spinners, we theoretically have the same probability of 
 results for both spinners, but we may still get different results
Experimental probability is determining probability based on review of the results of 
an experiment that has been carried out many times.
5.SP.2.3
Understand the concept of theoretical probability and how it 
relates to experimental probability, developing proficiency in 
applying theoretical concepts to practical situations.
 
Notice if the student:
• Understands the concept of theoretical probability and can explain it in their own 
words.
• Relates theoretical probability to experimental probability by comparing expected 
outcomes to actual results.
• Calculates theoretical probabilities for simple events.
• Applies theoretical probability concepts to predict outcomes in practical situations.
• Communicates the differences between theoretical and experimental probability 
clearly and effectively.
Additional notes:
Provide activities where students first calculate theoretical probabilities (e.g., rolling a 
die) and then conduct experiments to compare with actual outcomes. Use visual aids 
like probability trees and tables to help students understand and calculate theoretical 
probabilities.
Foster discussions on why theoretical and experimental probabilities might differ, 
highlighting factors like sample size and random variation.
Grade 5 ◊ Statistics and Probability

Mathematics Curriculum 4.149
GRADE 6: NUMBER
Guiding Question: How do we use fractions, percentages, and decimals in daily life?
Learning 
Concept 
Students will 
learn about:
Learning Outcome
By the end of Grade 6, students will:
1. Number sense 6.N.1.1
Express a part of a whole as a percentage in a variety of ways.
 
Notice if the student:
• Determines a given percentage represented by concrete and pictorial models and 
 records it symbolically.
• Represents a given percentage concretely and pictorially.
• Estimates percentages (for example, percent grade on a test or percent of interest 
 that would be owed on an item).
Additional notes:
Percentage is a part compared to a whole. When calculating percentage, the whole 
is represented as 100.
A percentage can be written as a ratio, a fraction, or a decimal.
When beginning to work with percentages, initial models should involve 
manipulatives that have 100 parts. 
To estimate means to find an approximate answer or a value close to the right answer. 

4.150
6.N.1.2
Compare and order fractions, decimals, and percentages to 
thousandths.
 
Notice if the student:
• Expresses percentages, fractions, and decimals interchangeably (within 
 thousandths).
• Arranges combinations of fractions, decimals, and percentages in ascending and 
 descending order.
• Compares fractions, decimals, and percentages using the greater than (>), less 
 than (<), and equal (=) symbols.
• Rounds decimal numbers to the nearest thousandth.
• Recognizes and records decimals to thousandths in expanded form.
Additional notes:
A fraction is part of a whole. For example, -
1 
4
 of a dollar is a quarter or 25 cents, 
-
5 
10 is half of 10.
A decimal acts as a separation of a whole number from its fractional part.
Percentage is a part compared to a whole. When calculating percentage, the whole is 
represented as one hundred.
When expressing percentages, fractions, and decimals interchangeably, students 
should be able to show the amount in all three ways. 
For example: 90%, 90/100, or 0.9
Ascending order means to list values from least to greatest.
Descending order means to list values from greatest to least.
Rounding means to replace a number with a simpler number, keeping the value close 
to what it was originally. When rounding with decimals, students are encouraged to 
use manipulatives, such as decimal strips and place-value lines. To round a number, 
take a look at the digit you are rounding and look to the right of that digit. If the digit 
to the right is 5 or greater, round the digit to the left up. If it's less than 5, the digit 
stays the same. For example, with the number 4.825, if you are rounding to the nearest 
tenth, the number becomes 4.8. If you are rounding to the nearest hundredth, the 
number becomes 4.83.
Using expanded form means to rewrite the digits of a number based on their place. 
values (for example, 254 = 200 + 50 + 4).
Grade 6 ◊ Number

Mathematics Curriculum 4.151
6.N.1.3
Demonstrate equivalent fractions for any given fraction.
 
Notice if the student:
• Uses visual models to show equivalent fractions.
• Creates equivalent fractions by multiplying the numerator and denominator of a 
 given fraction by the same number. 
• Compares equivalent fractions.
• Explains why two fractions are equivalent.
• Represents mixed numbers as improper fractions and vice versa.
Additional notes:
A fraction is part of a whole. For example, -
1 
4
 of a dollar is a quarter or 25 cents, 
-
5 
10 is half of 10.
Equivalent fractions are fractions that represent the same location on a number 
line or the same part of the same whole. Every fraction has an infinite number of 
equivalent fractions.
The numerator is the number in a fraction that shows the number of parts out of 
the whole. It is the top number in a fraction.
The denominator is the number in a fraction that shows the number of equal 
parts that the whole is divided into. It is the bottom number in a fraction.
Any fraction can be changed into an equivalent fraction by multiplying the 
numerator and denominator by the same number.

4.152 Grade 6 ◊ Number
6.N.1.4
Represent and interpret simple ratios.
 
Notice if the student:
• Uses a ratio to describe the relationship between two quantities.
• Represents ratios concretely and pictorially. 
• Expresses ratios as part-part and as part-whole. 
• Finds equivalent ratios.
• Uses simple ratios to represent real-life situations.
Additional notes:
A ratio is a comparison between two quantities.
Part-part ratios compare two parts of something. 
Part-whole ratios compare part of something to the whole thing.
Equivalent ratios represent the same relationship (are equal).
Students can find equivalent ratios concretely and symbolically by, for example, 
drawing or using ratio tables. A ratio table can be used to represent pairs of 
corresponding values, with an equivalent ratio between each pair.

Mathematics Curriculum 4.153
2. Operations 6.N.2.1
Add and subtract whole numbers with answers up to 1,000,000 
and decimal numbers to thousandths. 
 
Notice if the student:
• Makes reasonable estimates involving addition and subtraction of whole numbers 
 and decimals.
• Fluently adds and subtracts whole numbers and decimals to thousandths 
 (for example, calculations involving money or interest).
• Relates the strategy used to a written method and explains the reasoning used.
• Chooses efficient methods of operations when adding and subtracting whole 
 and decimal numbers to thousandths. 
Additional notes:
A whole number is a number that does not contain fractional parts or decimals.
A decimal acts as a separation of a whole number from its fractional part.
To estimate means to find an approximate answer or a value close to the right 
answer. 
Students should continue to use concrete models, drawings, and strategies based on 
place value when solving problems. 
6.N.2.2
Identify common factors and multiples of whole numbers. 
 
Notice if the student:
• Finds factor pairs for whole numbers to 100.
• Determines the multiples of a given number.
• Determines prime and composite numbers.
• Uses prior knowledge of multiplication facts to solve for factors and multiples.
Additional notes:
Factors are numbers that can divide evenly into other numbers (for example, the 
factors of 8 are 1, 2, 4, and 8).
A multiple is the product of a number multiplied by another number (for example, 
the multiples of 8 are 8, 16, 24, 32, etc)
A whole number is a number that does not contain fractional parts or decimals.

4.154 Grade 6 ◊ Number
A prime number is a number with exactly two factors: itself and 1 (for example, 2, 
3, 5, 7, 11, 13, 17, and 19).
A composite number is a number with three or more factors (for example, 4, 6, 8, 9, 
10, 12, 14, and 15).
6.N.2.3
Multiply decimal numbers by one-digit whole numbers in daily 
life contexts.
 
Notice if the student:
• Makes reasonable estimates involving multiplying a decimal by a whole number.
• Uses a variety of strategies to solve a given multiplication problem involving 
 decimals (for example, using area models and standard algorithms).
• Places the decimal point correctly in a total amount.
Additional notes:
A decimal acts as a separation of a whole number from its fractional part.
A whole number is a number that does not contain fractional parts or decimals.
To estimate means to find an approximate answer or a value close to the right 
answer.
A standard algorithm is a series of steps that is used to solve a mathematical 
problem.
Activities could include multiplying the same whole numbers by 0.1, 0.01, etc., and 
identifying patterns.
For example:
8 x 1 = 8
8 x 0.1 = 0.8
8 x 0.01 = 0.08
8 x 0.001 = 0.008
When multiplying a whole number by a decimal, the placement of the decimal 
point is very important.
Daily life contexts may include calculations involving money, sports statistics, and 
measurements.
An area model is a diagram representing fractions as part of an area or region.

Mathematics Curriculum 4.155
6.N.2.4
Represent and solve division problems that involve dividing 
decimal numbers by single-digit whole numbers. 
 
Notice if the student:
• Makes reasonable estimates involving decimals. 
• Represents division problems involving decimals concretely and pictorially.
• Places the decimal point in the correct position in the quotient.
• Solves problems involving dividing decimals using a variety of strategies, 
 including standard algorithms.
Additional notes:
A decimal acts as a separation of a whole number from its fractional part.
A whole number is a number that does not contain fractional parts or decimals.
To estimate means to find an approximate answer or a value close to the right 
answer. 
The quotient is the number that results when you divide one number by another. 
A divisor is a number by which another number is divided in an equation. When 
you divide 21 by 7, 7 is the divisor.
A dividend is the number being divided in an equation. When you divide 21 by 7, 21 
is the dividend.
A standard algorithm is a series of steps that is used to solve a mathematical 
problem.
6.N.2.5
Apply order of operations to evaluate numerical expressions 
(excluding exponents) involving whole numbers. 
 
Notice if the student:
• Explains or describes a numerical expression.
• Applies rules for order of operations (excluding exponents) to solve multi-step 
 problems.
• Evaluates a numerical expression. 
Additional notes:
When teaching order of operations, instruction lessons should be carefully 
sequenced from simple to more complex problems. 
The order of operation rules tell us the correct sequence of steps to follow in 
evaluating an expression.

4.156 Grade 6 ◊ Number
To evaluate a numerical expression means to find the value of the expression when 
the variable is replaced by a given numerical value. We substitute the given number 
for the variable in the expression and then simplify the expression using order of 
operations.
Operations inside brackets should be done first, followed by multiplication and 
division from left to right, and then addition and subtraction, also from left to right.
A numerical expression is a mathematical statement containing only numbers 
and at least one operation.
In this grade, evaluating expressions should not include exponents. The exponent
refers to how many times a number is to be multiplied by itself. It is sometimes 
called the power of a number.
A whole number is a number that does not contain fractional parts or decimals.

Mathematics Curriculum 4.157
GRADE 6: PATTERNS AND RELATIONS
Guiding Question: How can we use patterns in data to make decisions?
Learning 
Concept 
Students will 
learn about:
Learning Outcome
By the end of Grade 6, students will:
1. Patterns 6.PR.1.1
Represent increasing and decreasing patterns in graphs.
 
Notice if the student:
• Constructs a graph from a given table of values.
• Translates an increasing or decreasing pattern to a table of values, and graphs the 
 table of values. 
• Explains how a table of values and a graph are related.
• Creates a table of values from a graph.
Additional notes:
An increasing pattern is a pattern that gets bigger in the same way repeatedly.
A decreasing pattern is a pattern that gets smaller in the same way repeatedly.
To translate means to create an alternate representation.
Students should be working with only linear (straight-line) graphs, in the first 
quadrant of the Cartesian plane.
Key features of graphs and tables include the starting value and how the graph or 
table increases or decreases.

4.158 Grade 6 ◊ Patterns and Relations
A table of values is a graphic organizer or chart that helps students determine two 
or more points that can be used to create a graph.
6.PR.1.2
Describe patterns in graphs that use increasing or decreasing 
patterns.
 
Notice if the student:
• Describes the relationships shown on a graph.
• Identifies and corrects errors on a graph.
Additional notes:
An increasing pattern is a pattern that gets bigger in the same way repeatedly.
A decreasing pattern is a pattern that gets smaller in the same way repeatedly.
Students will focus on graphs in the first quadrant of the Cartesian plane with 
whole-number values. 
6.PR.1.3
Use function tables to represent and analyze patterns, 
identifying the input-output relationships and recognizing

 in a table of values, using a mathematical expression.
• Uses a general rule to generate a corresponding table of values.
• Identifies the general rule of an increasing or decreasing pattern.
• Uses an algebraic expression with variables to demonstrate the general rule.

The general rule helps us find the value of any term in a pattern (for example, in a

Additional notes:
The general rule helps us find the value of any term in a pattern (for example, in a 
chart, column 1 is A, and column 2 is B. We determine the general rule is A x 3 = B).
An increasing pattern is a pattern that gets bigger in the same way repeatedly.
A decreasing pattern is a pattern that gets smaller in the same way repeatedly.
Students should use charts, tables, and graphs related to their lives, the land, and 
local or territorial data to make meaningful connections.
Students should make near and far predictions and extend their graphs to verify.

4.160 Grade 6 ◊ Patterns and Relations
2. Expressions 
and equations
6.PR.2.1
Explain the concept of coefficients in expressions, recognizing 
them as the numerical factors attached to variables.
 
Notice if the student:
• Substitutes a known whole number or decimal value into a given expression.
• Simplifies an expression.
Additional notes:
A mathematical expression is a statement with at least two numbers or variables 
and at least one operation.
For example:
x + 3
m - 3
A whole number is a number that does not contain fractional parts or decimals.
A decimal acts as a separation of a whole number from its fractional part.
A variable is a letter or term that represents an unknown number, value, or 
quantity.
To evaluate an expression, we replace a variable with a known value and simplify.
For example:
Find the solution to y + 0.4 if y = 0.2.
y = 0.2, therefore 0.2 + 0.4 = 0.6.
6.PR.2.2
Solve and verify one-step equations, including equations with 
whole-number coefficients and solutions.
 
Notice if the student:
• Solves one-step equations by performing inverse operations.
• Checks to verify answers.
Additional notes:
A coefficient is a number that is used to multiply a variable.
Example:
4x = 16, therefore x = 4
5p = 50, therefore p = 10
An inverse operation is a set of two opposite operations that undo each other (for
example, 5 + 3 = 8, and 8 – 3 = 5).

Mathematics Curriculum 4.161

4.162
GRADE 6: SHAPE AND SPACE
Guiding Question: When are precise measurements necessary?
Learning 
Concept 
Students will 
learn about:
Learning Outcome
By the end of Grade 6, students will:
1. Measurement 6.SS.1.1
Solve problems involving the perimeter of complex shapes. 
 
Notice if the student:
• Finds the perimeter of a complex shape when given all side lengths.
• Finds the length of the missing side given the perimeter and the other side lengths.
• Breaks complex shapes into simple shapes to simplify solving for perimeter.
• Combines complex shapes and then recalculates the new perimeter.
Additional notes:
Perimeter is the distance around a shape along its border.
A complex shape is a shape made up of two or more simple shapes.
Length refers to the measurement of something from end to end.
Shapes, including parts of circles, are not included in this grade.
6.SS.1.2
Solve problems involving the area of triangles, parallelograms, 
and trapezoids.
 
Notice if the student:
• Demonstrates how the area of a rectangle can be used to determine the area of 
 a triangle.
• Demonstrates how the area of a rectangle can be used to determine the area of 
 a parallelogram.
• Calculates the area of a parallelogram using the standard algorithm.
• Calculates the area of a triangle using the standard algorithm.
• Demonstrates how the areas of a rectangle and triangle can be used to determine 
 the area of a trapezoid.
• Calculates the area of a trapezoid.
Grade 6 ◊ Shape and Space

Mathematics Curriculum 4.163
Additional notes:
Area is the amount of space taken up by a 2D figure. Area is measured in square 
units (for example, square millimetres, square centimetres, square metres, etc)
The area of a parallelogram is equal to its base times its height.
A = bh
b = base h = height
The area of a triangle is equal to its base times its height, divided by 2.
A = 1/2 × b × h
b = base h = height
A triangle is a shape with three sides.
A parallelogram is a shape with two sets of parallel lines.
A trapezoid is a shape with four sides and at least two parallel lines.
A standard algorithm is a series of steps that is used to solve a mathematical 
problem.
6.SS.1.3
Estimate and measure volume by counting unit cubes.
 
Notice if the student:
• Identifies personal referents for a cubic centimetre and a cubic metre.
• Estimates the volume of given 3D figures using personal referents.
• Constructs rectangular prisms for given volumes.
• Finds the volume of rectangular prisms.
• Compares 3D objects to determine which has greater or less volume.
Additional notes:
To estimate means to find an approximate answer or a value close to the right 
answer. 
Volume is the amount of space taken up by a 3D object.
A cube is a 3D object with six square faces, 12 equal straight edges, and eight vertices.
A referent is an object or known value that can be used to help us estimate a 
measurement or amount.
Personal referents are objects students see or use often and that can help 
them estimate the measurement of an unknown object. Students use personally 
meaningful referents to visualize units of measurement. They can use a unit in a 
set of base-ten blocks as a personal referent for a cubic centimetre.

4.164
A cubic centimetre is the volume of a cube that measures 1 centimetre × 1 
centimetre × 1 centimetre.
A cubic metre is the volume of a cube that measures 1 metre x 1 metre x 1 metre.
A prism is a solid 3D figure that has two congruent parallel faces.
6.SS.1.4
Measure and construct angles.
 
Notice if the student:
• Labels parts of an angle.
• Uses a protractor to measure angles in various orientations.
• Draws a given angle using a protractor.
Additional notes:
A protractor is a measurement tool used to measure an angle. 
An angle is where two lines meet or intersect.
Angles are measured in degrees.

Mathematics Curriculum 4.165
6.SS.1.5
Identify angles as right, straight, acute, obtuse, or reflex.
 
Notice if the student:
• Identifies angles in daily life that are right, straight, acute, obtuse, or reflex.
• Estimates the size of an angle using referents of 45, 90, and 180 degrees.
• Classifies angles according to their measurement.
Additional notes:
An angle is where two lines meet or intersect.
A reflex angle is an angle that measures more than 180° but less than 360°.
A right angle is an angle that measures 90 degrees—a “perfect” corner.
A straight angle is an angle that measures 180 degrees.
An acute angle is an angle that measures more than 0 degrees but less than 90 
degrees.
An obtuse angle is an angle that is greater than 90 degrees but less than 180 
degrees.
To estimate means to find an approximate answer or a value close to the right answer. 
A referent is an object or known value that can be used to help us estimate a 
measurement or amount.
6.SS.1.6
Solve problems involving converting units of time.
 
Notice if the student:
• Converts hours to minutes and minutes to seconds.
• Calculates elapsed time to the nearest minute.
• Calculates the number of hours over multiple days (for example, 24 x 2 = 48, so 
 that means the weekend is 48 hours long).
• Calculates the number of months, weeks, or years of an event (for example, if a 
 baby is 18 months old, this means they are 1 year and 6 months).
Acute angle Right angle Obtuse angle Straight angle Reflex angle

4.166
Additional notes:
Elapsed time refers to the amount of time that passes from the start of an event to 
its finish.
6.SS.1.7
Tell time, including 24-hour time systems, using analog and 
digital clocks.
 
Notice if the student:
• Reads and interprets the 24-hour clock.
• Tells time to the minute using 12- and 24-hour clocks.
• Converts time between 12- and 24-hour formats.

Activities for teaching the 24-hour clock can include using timetables, posted hours 
for the local co-op, and airport schedules.

• Sorts triangles into sets and explains the sorting rule.
• Classifies triangles as equilateral, isosceles, or scalene.
Additional notes:
A triangle is a shape with three sides.
A set is a collection of objects or items.
A sorting rule is a specific way to arrange or group objects based on their 
attributes.
Triangles can be sorted or classified into sets based on the length of the sides and 
the size of the angles.
An angle is where two lines meet or intersect.
An equilateral triangle is a three-sided shape with three equal sides and three 
equal angles that each measure 60 degrees.
An isosceles triangle is a three-sided shape where two sides are equal in length 
and two angles are the same degrees.
A scalene triangle is a three-sided shape with no equal sides or angles.

4.168
3. Transformations 6.SS.3.1
Plot and read points (coordinates) of a coordinate plane.
 
Notice if the student:
• Identifies x-axis, y-axis, and origin.
• Plots points by starting at the origin and moving along the x-axis before moving 
 parallel to the y-axis.
• Describes an ordered pair by listing the x coordinate before the y coordinate.
Additional notes:
A coordinate plane (also known as a Cartesian plane) is a grid containing four 
quadrants where points can be plotted to show data. 
The x-axis is the horizontal line on the graph from left to right that passes through 
the origin.
The y-axis is the vertical line on the graph from down to up that passes through 
the origin.
The origin is the point where the x and y axes meet (coordinate (0,0)).
An ordered pair (also referred to as coordinates) is a set of two numbers used to 
locate a point in a coordinate plane. It is read in the format (x, y)—x is always the 
first value in the ordered pair and y is always the second value in the ordered pair.

Mathematics Curriculum 4.169
6.SS.3.2
Carry out single transformations on 2D shapes in the first 
quadrant of the coordinate plane.
 
Notice if the student:
• Plots points on the coordinate plane.
• Performs a transformation on a 2D shape given the image and the 
 transformation.
• Describes a transformation on the coordinate plane given the pre-image and 
 image.
Additional notes:
Transformation involves moving an object from its original position to a new 
position. Transformations include reflection (flip), rotation (turn), and translation 
(slide).
A coordinate plane (also known as a Cartesian plan) is a grid containing four 
quadrants where points can be plotted to show data. 
To describe a translation (slide), students describe the direction of the slide.
To describe a reflection (flip), students describe the line of reflection (mirror line).
To describe a rotation (turn), students describe the direction of the turn.
The image shows the shape after a transformation.
The pre-image shows the shape before a transformation.

1. Data analysis 6.SP.1.1

• Uses various sampling techniques when collecting data.
• Considers factors in selecting a sample group and a type of sampling.
Additional notes:
Second-hand data is information that is collected by someone else. The student 
is not part of creating and answering the question. Examples include information 
gathered from government data, news, and sports statistics.
In this grade, students should be exploring first- and second-hand data.
Population refers to the entire group that we want to draw conclusions about.
A sample is a subset of the population (a part of the whole group).
The results from a sample can be used to make generalizations about the 
population.

4.172 Grade 6 ◊ Statistics and Probability
6.SP.1.3
Read and interpret line graphs. 
 
Notice if the student:

 graphs.

Additional notes:
Line graphs make it easy to see trends over time. The points plotted on a line graph 
show the relationship between the variables represented.
Frequency is the number of times an event occurs.
A frequency table is a table that lists data items and shows the number of times each 
occurs.
A legend is a tool that helps explain the values in a graph. It shows the ways the 
numbers or pictures are used to represent data. 
6.SP.1.4
Use statistical methods to analyze data, identify trends, and make 
predictions, applying advanced analytical techniques to draw 
meaningful insights from data.
 
Notice if the student:
• Uses statistical methods to analyze data, identifying trends and patterns accurately.
• Applies advanced analytical techniques to draw meaningful insights from the data.

predictions.
• Demonstrates understanding of different statistical methods and when to use them.
• Communicates their findings and predictions clearly, using appropriate statistical 
terminology.
Additional notes:
Provide students with real-world data sets relevant to their community or interests, 
such as weather patterns, local wildlife populations, or community survey results.
Integrate cultural examples, such as analyzing historical data on traditional Inuit 
practices or environmental changes, to make the lessons more relevant
Encourage students to conduct their own research projects, collecting and analyzing 
data to identify trends and make predictions.
Foster discussions on the implications of their findings, such as how predicting trends 
can aid in planning and decision-making in their community.
