2. Chance and 
uncertainty
6.SP.2.1
Represent the probabilities of independent events in a variety of 
ways, including fractions, decimals, and percentages.
 
Notice if the student:
• Represents probabilities as fractions, decimals, and percentages on a number line.
• Determines when it is most appropriate to represent probabilities as a fraction, 
 decimal, or percentage. 
Additional notes:
A fraction is part of a whole. For example, -
1 
4
 of a dollar is a quarter or 25 cents, 
-
5 
10 is half of 10.
A decimal acts as a separation of a whole number from its fractional part.
Percentage is a part compared to a whole. When calculating percentage, the whole is 
represented as 100.
When students become comfortable with percentages, they can use 0% to represent 
impossible events (0) and 100% to represent certain events (1).

4.174
6.SP.2.2
Predict and test the probability of two independent events 
through single-outcome experiments.
 
Notice if the student:
• Lists the possible outcomes of two independent events.
• Designs and carries out experiments to test the likelihood of two independent 
 events.
• Compares the theoretical and experimental probabilities of single-outcome events.
Additional notes:
Probability refers to how likely an event is to happen.
Examples of two independent events may include rolling two dice or spinning two 
spinners.
Theoretical probability refers to what we expect to happen based on the 
mathematical probability.
For example:
• Whether you roll a die six times or 60 times, the probability of rolling a 1 is still 
 1/6.
• Theoretically we have a 1 in 6 chance of rolling a 1 on a die, but it is possible our 
 actual results may be more or less than 1/6. 
• If we spin two identical spinners, we theoretically have the same probability of 
 results for both spinners, but we may still get different results.
Experimental probability is determining probability based on review of the results 
of an experiment that has been carried out many times.
 
Grade 6 ◊ Statistics and Probability

Mathematics Curriculum 4.175
6.SP.2.3
Use probability models to solve problems and make predictions, 
demonstrating proficiency in applying probability concepts to 
practical scenarios.
 
Notice if the student:
• Uses probability models accurately to solve problems and make predictions.
• Demonstrates proficiency in applying probability concepts to practical scenarios.
• Interprets the results of probability models and explains their implications.
• Chooses appropriate probability models for different types of problems.
• Communicates their problem-solving process and predictions clearly using 
correct probability terminology.
Additional notes:
A probability model is a model that describes and analyze the likelihood 
of different events. A probability model is a convenient way to describe the 
distribution of the outcomes of an experiment.
Encourage collaborative projects where students work in groups to solve complex 
problems using probability models and present their findings.
Discuss the limitations of different probability models and the importance of 
considering multiple factors when making predictions.
Refer to Mathematics Educator Handbook for more information. 



ᐅᖃᐅᓰᑦ ᑐᑭᖏᑦ
UQAUSIIT TUKINGIT
GLOSSARY



Mathematics Curriculum 5.1
ᐅᖃᐅᓰᑦ ᑐᑭᖏᑦ 
UQAUSIIT TUKINGIT
GLOSSARY 
The following definitions and examples are intended to help teachers use 
this document. The examples provided are suggestions and are not meant to 
be exhaustive.
ab pattern
A pattern with two distinct elements in its core that repeat (for example, 
blue, red, blue, red, blue, red).
abc pattern
A pattern with three distinct elements in its core that repeat (for 
example, blue, red, green, blue, red, green, blue, red, green).
acute angle
An angle that measures more than 0 degrees but less than 90 degrees.
addend
A number that is added to another number.
angle
Where two lines meet or intersect.
area
The amount of space taken up by a 2D figure. Area is measured in square 
units (for example, square millimetres, square centimetres, square 
metres, etc)
area model
A diagram representing fractions as part of an area or region.
ascending order
Listing values from least to greatest. 

5.2 Glossary
associative property of addition
No matter how a set of three or more numbers are grouped together, the 
sum remains the same. The grouping of numbers is done with the help 
of brackets.
associative property of multiplication
The idea that, if we have three or more numbers to multiply, we can 
multiply them in any grouping that we want, and this does not change 
the result.
attribute
A characteristic of an object, sound, or action, such as shape, colour or size.
• Attributes of shapes and objects: faces, vertices, edges, and angles
• Attributes of patterns: colours, direction, shape, and size.
bar graph
A graph that presents data with rectangular bars proportional to 
the values that they represent. The bars can be drawn vertically or 
horizontally.
benchmarks
Natural stopping points when counting or comparing numbers (for 
example, 7 is 5 and 2 more).
capacity
The amount that something can hold.
cardinality
The last count word indicates the number of objects in the set. For 
example, when counting three objects, the student knows that when 
they have counted the last object they have their total, which is three.
Carroll diagram
An organizer used to sort a group of objects or shapes based on given 
properties or traits in a yes-or-no fashion.
coefficient
A number that is used to multiply a variable.

Mathematics Curriculum 5.3
commutative property of addition
The idea that the order in which we add a set of numbers does not 
change the result.
commutative property of multiplication
The idea that the order in which we multiply a set of numbers does 
change the result.
compose
To combine numbers to create a larger number.
complex shape
A shape made up of two or more simple shapes.
composite number
A number with three or more factors, (for example, 4, 6, 8, 9, 10, 12, 14, and 15).
concrete graph
A graph that records, organizes, and displays data using tangible, handson objects. When using objects to create concrete graphs, it is best to 
arrange each object in a grid, and it is important that the concrete 
objects used be the same shape and size.
concrete objects
Physical resources that students can handle and manipulate to aid 
their understanding of different math concepts. Examples may include 
counters, rocks, and base-ten blocks. 
cone
A 3D object with one circular face, one curved edge, one curved surface, 
and one apex (the top of the cone, opposite the base).

5.4 Glossary
conservation of number
The idea that the number of objects in a set remains the same regardless of 
the order in which they are counted or the arrangement of the objects.
coordinate plane
A grid containing quadrants where points can be plotted to show data. Also 
known as a Cartesian plane.
core
The shortest part of the pattern that repeats.
cube
A 3D object with six square faces, 12 equal straight edges, and eight vertices.
cubic centimetre
The volume of a cube that measures 1 centimetre × 1 centimetre × 1 
centimetre.
cubic metre
The volume of a cube that measures 1 metre x 1 metre x 1 metre.
cuboid
A 3D object with six faces (four or six rectangular faces), 12 straight edges, and 
eight vertices.
cylinder
A 3D object with two circular faces, one curved surface, and two curved edges.

Mathematics Curriculum 5.5
data set
The information that has been collected.
descending order
Listing values from greatest to least.
decimal
Acts as a separation of a whole number from its fractional part.
decompose
To break a number down into smaller numbers.
decreasing pattern
A pattern that gets smaller in the same way repeatedly.
denominator
The number in a fraction that shows the number of equal parts that the 
whole is divided into. It is the bottom number in a fraction.
difference
the result of subtracting one number from another.
double-bar graph
A graph used to show two sets of data on the same graph.
doubling
Adding a number to itself to double the amount (for example, 100 + 100 = 
200).
doubles
Adding the same number together (for example, 1 + 1, 2 + 2, etc). 
Automatically knowing doubles helps us do mental math quicker.
elapsed time
The amount of time that passes from the start of an event to its finish.
elements
The parts of a pattern—the actual objects, sounds, actions, or items used 
in a pattern, such as squares, cubes, or claps. 

5.6 Glossary
estimate
To find an approximate answer or a value close to the right answer. 
equilateral triangle
A three-sided shape with three equal sides and three equal angles that 
measure 60 degrees.
dividend
a number being divided in an equation. When you divide 21 by 7, 21 is the 
dividend.
divisor
a number by which another number is divided in an equation. When 
you divide 21 by 7, 7 is the divisor.
equivalent fractions
Fractions that represent the same location on a number line or the 
same part of the same whole. Every fraction has an infinite number of 
equivalent fractions. 
equivalent ratios
Ratios that represent the same relationship (are equal).
equivalent patterns
Patterns that have the same core structure (for example, red-blue-redblue-red-blue is the same core structure as green-yellow-green-yellowgreen-yellow).
expanded form
Rewriting the digits of a number based on their place values (for 
example, 254 = 200 + 50 + 4).
experimental probability
Determining probability based on review of the results of an experiment 
that has been carried out many times
60° 60°
60°

Mathematics Curriculum 5.7
exponent
How many times a number is to be multiplied by itself. It is sometimes 
called the power of a number. Exponents are not explored in 
Kindergarten to grade 6.
expression
A math statement with at least two different numbers (known or 
unknown) and at least one operation.
For example:
x + 3
m - 8
even numbers
Whole numbers that can be divided evenly into pairs or divided by 2 
evenly.
factors
Numbers that can divide evenly into other numbers (for example, the 
factors of 8 are 1, 2, 4, and 8).
first-hand data
Information that is collected by oneself or a group of people. The person 
or people are involved in both the creation of questions and collection of 
data.
fraction
A part of a whole. For example, For example, 1/4 of a dollar is a quarter or 
25 cents, 5/10 is half of 10. Any fraction can be changed into an equivalent 
fraction by multiplying the numerator and denominator by the same 
number.
fractional notation
An amount written as a fraction, with a numerator and denominator.
frequency
The number of times an event occurs.

5.8 Glossary
frequency table
A table that lists data items and shows the number of times each occurs.
friendly numbers
Numbers that end in 0.
front-end addition
Adding numbers of two or more digits by starting with the digits of the 
largest place value, and then adding the remaining sums. For example, 24 
+ 35 = (20 + 30) + (4 + 5).
general rule
Rule that helps us find the value of any term in a pattern (for example, 
in a chart, column 1 is A, and column 2 is B. We determine the general 
rule is A x 3 = B).
increasing pattern
A pattern that gets bigger in the same way repeatedly. 
inverse operations
A set of two opposite operations that undo each other (for example, 
5 + 3 = 8, and 8 – 3 = 5).
irregular polygon
A 2D shape with sides and angles that are not all equal (for example, a 
rectangle).
isosceles triangle
A three-sided shape where two sides are equal in length and two angles 
are the same degrees.
= =

Mathematics Curriculum 5.9
legend
A tool that helps explain the values in a graph. It shows the ways the 
numbers or pictures are used to represent data.
length
Measurement of something from end to end.
line of symmetry
The line that divides a shape into identical halves.
making 10
Rounding up or down to 10 in order to make an equation easier to solve 
(for example, I don’t know 9 + 3, but I can give 1 to the 9 to make 10 and 
solve as 10 + 2, which is simpler).
manipulatives
Objects and tools that can be held and moved to show or assist in solving 
math concepts. Examples include ten frames, hundred charts, base-ten 
blocks, coins, counters, and linking cubes.
many-to-one correspondence
Representing many objects by one object or interval on a graph (for 
example, in a pictograph, 1 smiley face can represent 5 students).
measurable attribute
A characteristic of a shape that can be measured, such as its length, 
mass, and capacity.
mental math
To do calculations in one’s head using a variety of strategies to solve a 
problem. Strategies may include recalling doubles and near doubles, 
making 10, doubles plus one, and thinking addition to subtract. See the 
Mathematics Educator Handbook for more information on mental math 
strategies.

5.10 Glossary
minuend
the number in a subtraction sentence from which another number is 
subtracted. For example, in 15-9=6, 15 is the minuend.
multiple
The products of a number multiplied by another number (for example, 
the multiples of 8 are 8, 16, 24, 32, etc).
multi-attribute pattern
A pattern that contains more than one characteristic (for example, shape 
and size). Also known as a complex pattern.
multiplication tables
The mathematical operations of multiplying numbers from 0 to 12, 
which can be displayed on a table.
near doubles
Adding or subtracting two numbers where one number is only 1 more 
than the other by using doubles and then adding or subtracting 1 (for 
example, using 4 + 4 to solve 4 + 5. We now know 4 + 4 is 8, so we add one 
more to get 9 = 4 + 5).
net
A 2D shape that can be folded to make a 3D object.
non-standard units
Items used to measure something approximately, without referring to 
a standard common unit of measurement, such as centimetres. Nonstandard units may be uniform (always the same size) or non-uniform 
(differing in size). Uniform units may include interlocking cubes, base-ten 
blocks, and pattern blocks. Non-uniform units may include hands, feet, 
and pencils.
number bonds
Pairs of numbers that, when added together, result in a certain number.
3 3 2 4
6 6

Mathematics Curriculum 5.11
number sentence
A mathematical sentence that is made up of numbers and signs, 
indicating an equality or inequality (for example, 3 + 2 = 5).
numeral
The symbol used to represent a number. Also known as a digit. For 
example, 4 is the numeral for four.
numerator
The number in a fraction that shows the number of parts out of the 
whole. It is the top number in a fraction.
numerical correspondence
When the number of actual objects is accurately represented by the 
pictures on a graph or table.
numerical expression
A mathematical statement that contains only numbers and at least one 
operation.
obtuse angle
An angle that is greater than 90 degrees but less than 180 degrees.
odd numbers
Whole numbers that cannot be divided exactly into pairs, or divided by 2, 
without leaving a remainder.
one-to-one correspondence
To count one and only one object as you say the number (for example, 
touching a counter and moving it to a pile as you count).
ordinal numbers
An object’s position in a series, such as 1st, 2nd, 3rd, etc.

5.12 Glossary
ordered pair
A set of two numbers used to locate a point in a coordinate plane. It is 
read in the format (x, y)—x is always the first value and y is always the 
second value in the ordered pair. Also referred to as coordinates.
origin
The point where the x and y axes meet (coordinate (0,0)).
parallelogram
A shape with two sets of parallel lines.
part-part ratios
Ratios that compare two parts of something.
part-part-whole representation 
The relationship between a whole and its parts and refer to the idea that 
a number can be split or partitioned into component parts. Identifying 
part-part-whole relationships is a major milestone in early number 
sense.
part-whole ratios
Ratios that compare part of something to the whole thing.
pattern rule
How each element in a pattern behaves and the relationships among the 
elements in a pattern.
percentage
A part compared to a whole. When calculating percentage, the whole is 
represented as 100. A percentage can be written as a ratio, a fraction, or a 
decimal.
perimeter
The distance around a shape along its border.

Mathematics Curriculum 5.13
personal referents
Objects students see or use often and that can help them estimate the 
measurement of an unknown object. Students use personally meaningful 
referents to visualize units of measurement.
pictograph
A graph that uses pictures or symbols to show the value of data.
place value
The value a numeral holds based on its place in the number.
polygon
A closed shape with at least three sides and three angles.
population
The entire group that we want to draw conclusions about.
prime number
A number with exactly two factors: itself and 1. For example, 2, 3, 5, 7, 11, 13, 17, 
and 19.
prism
A solid 3D figure that has two congruent parallel faces.
probability
How likely an event is to happen.
protractor
A measurement tool used to measure an angle. 
pyramid
A solid 3D figure that has one base and at least three triangular sides.

5.14 Glossary
quadrilateral
A four-sided polygon. All quadrilaterals have four straight sides and four 
angles.
quotient
The number that results when you divide one number by another.
random sampling
A method of obtaining a sample of a population such that everyone in 
the population has an equal chance of being selected.
ratio
A comparison between two quantities. 
referent
An object or known value that can be used to help us estimate a 
measurement or amount.
reflex angle
An angle that measures more than 180 degrees but less that 360 degrees.
regrouping
To combine objects or amounts into groups of 10 to make adding and 
subtracting simpler.
regular polygon
A 2D shape that has all equal sides and angles (for example, an 
equilateral triangle, a square, or a regular hexagon).
>180º
< 360º

Mathematics Curriculum 5.15
relative position
The location of an object in relation to another object. For example, the 
triangle is beside the square, or the triangle is located to the right of the 
circle.
repeated addition
To add the same number repeatedly (for example, 6 + 6 = 12, 12 + 6 = 18, 
etc).
repeated subtraction
To subtract the same number from a larger number each time (for 
example, 36 – 6 = 30, 30 – 6 = 24, etc).
repeating pattern
A pattern in which a group of elements repeats itself as the pattern 
extends or continues. For example, ABABAB.
right angle
An angle that measures 90 degrees—a “perfect” corner.
rounding
To replace a number with a simpler number, keeping the value close to 
what it was originally. Numbers are typically rounded to multiples of 5, 
10, 100, 1000, etc).
sample
A subset of the population (a part of a whole group).
scalene triangle
A three-sided shape with no equal sides or angles.
second-hand data
Information that is collected by someone else. The student is not part of 
creating and answering the question(s). Examples include information 
gathered from government data, news, and sports statistics.

5.16 Glossary
set
A collection of objects or items.
skip counting
To count up repeatedly using the same number (for example, 10, 20, 30, 
40, etc).
sorting rule
A specific way to arrange or group objects based on their attributes.
sphere
A 3D object with one curved surface.
square unit
Equal to the area of a square with a side length of 1 unit.
standard algorithm
A series of steps used to solve a mathematical problem.
standard form
The way we normally write numbers (for example, 276 is written in 
standard form)
straight angle
An angle that measures 180 degrees.
subitize
To tell, at a glance, and without counting, the number of objects or items 
in a small group
subtrahend
The number that will be subtracted from another number.
survey
A method of collecting data by asking questions.

Mathematics Curriculum 5.17
symmetrical object
Any object or shape that can be cut into two equal parts.
table of values
A graphic organizer or chart that helps students determine two or more 
points that can be used to create a graph.
tally chart
A table with tally marks to show values in a data set.
theoretical probability
What we expect to happen based on the mathematical probability.
thinking addition to subtract
Changing simple subtraction facts into addition equations to solve them 
(for example, 9 – 7 is hard to solve, but I can think of it as addition as 7 + 
? = 9 and count on).
translate
To create an alternate representation.
transformation
Moving an object from its original position to a new position. These 
include reflection (flip), rotation (turn), and translation (slide).
trapezoid
A shape with four sides and at least two parallel lines.
triangle
A shape with three sides.
unit fraction
A fraction whose numerator is 1.

5.18 Glossary
variable
A letter or term that represents an unknown number, value, or quantity. 
Note that x, y, and z are the most common variables, but any variable 
can be used.
variable notation
A symbol that represents a value. For example: Find the solution to x + 6 
if the value of x = 3. (x = 3, therefore 3 + 6 = 9.)
venn diagram
A visual tool used to show the relationships between different sets. It 
consists of overlapping circles, where each circle represents a set. The 
areas where the circles overlap show the common elements shared by 
the sets, while the areas that do not overlap show the unique elements of 
each set.
verbal counting
To say the standard list of counting words in order.
vertex (vertices plural)
The common endpoint shared by two arms of an angle.
vertical addition
To place numbers on top of each other, adding the smallest numbers 
first and working from right to left. This method can involve carrying 
amounts.
volume
The amount of space taken up by a 3D object.
whole number
A number that does not contain fractional parts or decimals.
x-axis
The horizontal line on a graph from left to right that passes through the 
origin.

Mathematics Curriculum 5.19
y-axis
The vertical line on a graph from down to up that passes through the 
origin.
zero property of multiplication
The idea that whenever a number is multiplied by 0, the product is 
always 0.

Learning Strand: Number
The Number strand is the foundation for Mathematics. In number sense, students work toward developing 
an intuition about numbers and their relationships. They will develop and apply strategies to count and 
represent numbers and will explore the ways that numbers can be composed, decomposed, and utilized. In 
operations, students develop understanding of the concepts and procedures involved in operating on those numbers.

notice in numbers?

Overview of Number Learning Outcomes Kindergarten to Grade 6
Major Understandings:
1. Numbers help us describe, order, measure, quantify, and estimate so we can make sense of our 
world and solve everyday problems.
6.0 Overview of Number Learning Outcomes Kindergarten to Grade 6

2. Recognizing and creating patterns allows us to make predictions, describe and justify relationships, and make 
connections in our daily lives.
3. An understanding of the shapes and spaces around us allows us to explain, build, and navigate our environment. 
4. Collecting, representing, and interpreting information help us answer important questions and make informed 
decisions that benefit our families and communities.
Learning Strand: Number
Students learn how to model, apply, and develop strategies to add, subtract, multiply, and divide numbers. They also 
begin to develop a keen sense of how operations relate to each other. Understanding how to operate on numbers 
gives students a deeper understanding of the connectedness and usefulness of the number system and helps 
them build toward evaluating more abstract concepts of Mathematics that are introduced in later years.

Learning Strand: Patterns and Relations
Developing an understanding of patterns and relations is at the heart of learning Mathematics. 
Through this strand, students will identify, describe, extend, translate, and create a variety of 
patterns and produce generalizations about relationships within and between patterns.

Learning Strand: Patterns and Relations
Students will learn how patterns apply to sorting, numerical relationships, and computational 
applications. They will begin to understand algorithms. They will begin to understand how patterns, 
order, algorithms, expressions, and equations can be used to explain systems and solve problems.

Major Understandings:
1. Numbers help us describe, order, measure, quantify, and estimate so we can make sense of our world and 
solve everyday problems.
2. Recognizing and creating patterns allows us to make predictions, describe and justify relationships, and make 
connections in our daily lives.
3. An understanding of the shapes and spaces around us allows us to explain, build, and navigate our environment. 
4. Collecting, representing, and interpreting information help us answer important questions and make informed 
decisions that benefit our families and communities.
Learning Strand: Statistics and Probability
In this strand, students will explore how to collect, organize, and represent data in different ways. 
Students will begin to interpret data, noticing how it can contain patterns and frequencies that help 
us answer questions and how this information enables us to make informed predictions and decisions.

6.12 Overview of Statistics and Probability Learning Outcomes Kindergarten to Grade 6

Learning Strand: Statistics and Probability
Students will recognize how the quality of data directly affects the quality of the interpretations and will begin to explore how 
chance and uncertainty affect the likelihood of events. Students will develop an understanding of how asking questions, 
interpreting data, and determining probability empower us to work together to solve existing or potential problems.

Number Sense
Number Sense 
· Counting and Cardinality:

for counting activities
· Number Recognition: 
Identify and match 
numerals to quantities up 
to 20
· Basic Concepts: 
Understand the concept of 
more and less using Inuit

activities.
· Counting and Comparing: 
Count to 100 and compare 
numbers using more 
complex objects and images 
relevant to Inuit culture
· Number Sequencing: 
Arrange numbers in order 
up to 100, understanding the 
sequence of numbers
· Place Value Introduction: 
Begin to understand the 
concept of 10s and 1s with 
tangible examples like 
grouping beads or small 
rocks
· Counting and

and compare numbers 
using traditional and 
environmental items
· Place Value: Develop a 
deeper understanding of 
place value up to 1,000; use

· Number Patterns:

art

2s, 5s using visuals,

objects to 100

the given number

whole numbers up to

problems to 10 using simple

activities

with support

a problem

strategies to add and 
subtract whole numbers 
to 10

choosing efficient 
strategies
• Extends understanding of 
operations to using coins
• Associative property: 
when we add 3 or more

Number Learner Progressions, Kindergarten to Grade 6 6.23

Learning Strand: Patterns and Relations
Kindergarten Grade 1 Grade 2
Patterns
Patterns
· Pattern Recognition: 
Identify and describe 
simple patterns using 
everyday objects like 
stones, shells, and 
traditional items (e.g., AB, 
AAB, ABB patterns)
· Pattern Creation: Create 
simple repeating patterns 
using physical objects and 
drawings
· Cultural Integration: 
Explore patterns found in 
Inuit clothing, tools, and 
artwork
· Extending Patterns: 
Extend simple repeating 
patterns and describe 
them using terms like 
"repeat" and "core"
· Pattern Rules: 
Understand the rule of a 
pattern and predict what 
comes next
· Cultural Patterns: 
Identify and create 
patterns inspired by 
Inuit traditional designs, 
such as beading, qamutik 
building, and tapestry
· Complex Patterns: Create 
and extend more complex 
patterns (e.g., ABC, AABB, 
ABBA)
· Pattern Units: Identify the 
smallest unit of a pattern 
(the core) and describe it
· Cultural Relevance: Analyze 
patterns in traditional Inuit 
games and nature (e.g., 
animal tracks, cones, skins, 
snowflake structures)
Recognizing, Comparing, and Verbalizing
• Compares, understands, 
and identifies objects 
based on common 
attributes 
• Attribute is a 
characteristic of an 
object, sound, action (ex. 
shape, colour, size)
• Understands, 
demonstrates, extends, 
and fills in missing 
elements in repeating 
patterns in: sounds, 
movements, objects, 
routines
• Understands, describes, 
and sorts groups based 
on more than one 
attribute 
• Copies, extends, predicts, 
identifies, and creates 
missing elements of 
patterns
• Represents basic 
patterns in different 
ways (ex. sound, actions, 
objects)
• Uses manipulatives 
and found materials 
to construct and 
deconstruct patterns 
• Sorts objects into groups 
and explains sorting rule 
used based on at least two 
attributes with an ability to 
explain sorting rule
• Identifies core, extends, 
identifies missing parts and 
predicts future elements 
of repeating patterns with 
multiple attributes
• Identifies, describes 
similarities/differences and 
translates repeating patterns 
from one representation to 
another (colours to letters, 
sounds, actions, objects, 
symbols, etc.

6.28 Shape and Space Learner Progressions, Kindergarten to Grade 6
Learning Strand: Shape and Space
Kindergarten Grade 1 Grade 2
Measurement
Measurements
· Comparing Objects: 
Understand and compare 
sizes (big/small), lengths 
(long/short), weights 
(heavy/light), and 
capacities (full/empty) 
using everyday objects 
like stones and shells
· Non-Standard Units: 
Use non-standard units 
(e.g., hands, feet, sticks) 
to measure length and 
height
· Cultural Integration: 
Explore measurements in 
traditional activities, such 
as measuring fish at a 
derby or comparing sizes 
of tools
· Length and Height: 
Measure length and 
height using nonstandard units and begin 
to use standard units 
(centimetres and metres)
· Time: Understand the 
concept of time (morning, 
afternoon, evening) and 
measure time intervals 
using simple activities
· Cultural Examples: Use 
traditional Inuit stories 
and daily routines to 
discuss concepts of time 
and measurement
· Standard Units: Use 
standard units to measure 
length, height, and weight 
(centimeres, metres, grams, 
and kilograms)
· Tools: Introduce 
measurement tools like 
rulers, measuring tapes, and 
scales
· Cultural Context: Measure 
and compare objects found 
in nature and in daily life, 
such as animal tracks, fishing 
equipment, and traditional 
clothing
Measure, compare, and contrast
• Length: measurement of 
something from end to 
end
• Mass refers to how much 
of something there is.
• Capacity: refers to 
amount something holds
• Examines and describes 
objects using descriptive 
words (corners are pointy, 
etc.) 
• Compares/orders small 
groups of objects in 
ascending/descending 
order of length/height, 
mass, and capacity 
• Estimates length, height, 
weight of an object
• Units needed to fill 
object using nonstandard units 
• Finds objects lighter/
heavier than a given 
object
• Determines appropriate 
measurement (cm, m) to 
measure objects, selects 
tool (rulers, tape measures, 
meter sticks), records length 
accurately
• Draws line segments of a 
given length 
• Identifies meaningful 
references for 1 cm and 1 m 
and estimates lengths using 
these references

6.SS.3.1 Plot and read points (coordinates) of a 
coordinate plane.
6.SS.3.2 Carry out single transformations on 
2D shapes in the first quadrant of the 
coordinate plane. 
6.98 Mathematics Learning Outcomes checklists: Grade 6

Data Analysis 
 NOTES
6.SP.1.1 Design and conduct comprehensive data 
collection projects, including surveys and 
field studies, demonstrating advanced 
skills in planning and executing data 
collection processes. 
6.SP.1.2 Create and interpret advanced graphical 
representations, such as scatter plots 
and box plots, developing proficiency in 
visualizing and communicating complex 
data sets. 
6.SP.1.3 Read and interpret line graphs. 
6.SP.1.4 Use statistical methods to analyze data, 
identify trends, and make predictions, 
applying advanced analytical techniques 
to draw meaningful insights from data. 
6.SP.1.5 Analyze and interpret financial data from a 
business or local organization, making cost 
decisions and recommendations.
Mathematics Learning Outcomes checklists: Grade 6 6.99
Probability
 NOTES
6.SP.2.1 Represent the probabilities of 
independent events in a variety of 
ways, including fractions, decimals, and 
percentages. 
6.SP.2.2 Predict and test the probability of two 
independent events through singleoutcome experiments. 
6.SP.2.3 Use probability models to solve problems 
and make predictions, demonstrating 
proficiency in applying probability 
concepts to practical scenarios.
